\eta\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle k}=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle l}

for
\{i,j,k,l\}
a permutation of

\{1,2,3,4\}
. From (
17
) we have that

\Lambda_{\eta}
is the identity matrix for
\eta\in O.

We denote by
\mathbf{Z}
the vector
\overline{Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}}
.
The functional equation
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}=Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}|w
becomes:

\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{x};u)=\Lambda_{w}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{Z}(w\mathbf{x};u)

(18)

for all
w\in\widetilde{W}
, which follows from the cocycle relation of
\Lambda_{w}
.

4.2
An extension of the Chinta-Gunnells action

Define the transformation
\tau
acting on

f\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{x},u)
by

\tau f(\mathbf{x};u)=f\!\left(\mathbf{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right).

This transformation extends the natural action of

w\in\widetilde{W}
given by

wf(\mathbf{x};u)=f(w^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\mathbf{x};u)
,
and we denote by
\widetilde{W}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
the group
generated by
\widetilde{W}
and
\tau^{a}
,
a\in\mathbb{Z}
,
which can be seen as a subgroup of
\operatorname{End}(\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{x},u))
.
We show that there is an extension of the
Chinta-Gunnells action to this larger group

\widetilde{W}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, from which we will derive an extra
functional equation for the vector function
\mathbf{Z}
.
As in the previous subsection, we willrestrict the action to
the space
\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{x},u)_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
of functions
f
with
f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{o}$}}\!=0.

Similarly to the way the Chinta-Gunnells action is defined
in (
16
), we look for an action of the form

f|\tau(\mathbf{x};u)=A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}(\mathbf{x};u)\tau f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}(\mathbf{x};u)+A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}(\mathbf{x};u)\tau f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}(\mathbf{x};u)+A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{e}$}}(\mathbf{x};u)\tau f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{e}$}}(\mathbf{x};u)

(19)

defined for
f\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{x},u)_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
, for three
unknown functions
A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}
,
A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}
,
A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{e}$}}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{x},u)
.
In order for this action to preserve the space

\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{x},u)_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
, we require that

A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{o}$}}\!=0,\quad A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{e}$}}\!=0,\quad A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{e}$}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}\!=0.

We also require this action to be compatible with the
Chinta-Gunnells action, namely

f|\tau|w=f|w|\tau\qquad\text{(for $w\in\widetilde{W}$)}.

(20)

Formula (
19
) can be written:

\overline{f|\tau}(\mathbf{x};u)=A(\mathbf{x};u)\bar{f}\!\left(\mathbf{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)

(21)

for the
3\times 3
matrix

A(\mathbf{x};u)=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}}&\frac{1}{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\overline{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}}&\frac{1}{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\overline{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}A_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{e}$}}}\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(22)

Defining
\Lambda_{\tau}:=A
,
condition (
20
) is then equivalent to the fact that the
cocycle
\Lambda
has a well-defined exten-sion to the monoid

\widetilde{W}\times\{\tau^{n}\}_{n\geqslant 0}
by
the
1
-cocycle relation, namely it satisfies

\Lambda_{\tau w}=\Lambda_{w\tau}
for all

w\in\widetilde{W},
that is,

\Lambda_{w}(\mathbf{x};u)A(w\mathbf{x};u)=A(\mathbf{x};u)\Lambda_{w}\!\left(\mathbf{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right).

(23)

We also require that
A
is invertible,
so that
\Lambda_{\tau^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}}
is well-defined by the
cocycle relation. Therefore
f|\tau^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
iswell-defined as well, by the analogue of (
21
) written
for
\tau^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
, with
A
replaced by
\Lambda_{\tau^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}}
.

The main theorem of this section shows that there exists an extension
of the Chinta-Gunnells action to
\widetilde{W}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
as above, and
that
\mathbf{Z}
satisfies an additional functional equation under the
transformation
\tau
.

Theorem 4.2
.

—
There exists an invertible matrix
A(\mathbf{x};u)
of the form (
22
)
satisfying the following conditions:

\circ

The cocycle relation (
23
) is satisfied.

\circ

There exists an element
a\in\mathbb{C}\!\left(u,\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)
such that
the matrix
a\cdot A
has polynomial entries in
\mathbf{x}
and
u
.

\circ

The vector function
\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{x};u)
satisfies the functional
equation

\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{x};u)=A(\mathbf{x};u)\mathbf{Z}\!\left(\mathbf{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right).

(24)

In
[
27
]
, we will use results in
[
26
]
to show that a similar
result holds for arbitrary affine irreducible reduced root systems.

Remark 4.1
.

The matrix
A
in the theorem is essentially unique;
more precisely, any invertible matrix
A
ofthe form (
22
)
that satisfies the cocycle relation is unique, up to multiplication by
elements in
\mathbb{C}\!\left(u,\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)
. Weomit the proof of this fact
since it is quite technical, and we will not need it in the sequel.

Proof.

The relations (
23
), for
w
running through the generators
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
of
W
, reduce to a linear system of equations over the field
\mathbb{Q}(u)
,
with unknowns being the coefficients of the entries of
A
(assuming that these entries are
polynomials
of bounded degree in
\mathbf{x}
). Using MAGMA
[
3
]
, we solved this sparse system assuming that the degree of each entry is at most 16 in the variables
\mathbf{x}
(in which case there are about 100,000 unknowns and about four times as many equations).
We found an explicit non-singular matrix

A_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\mathbf{x};u)
of the form (
22
) with polynomial
entries in
\mathbf{x},u
satisfying (
23
), and the interested
reader can find it in
[
1
]
. Thus we have the corresponding
extensions of the cocycle
\Lambda
, and of the
Chinta-Gunnells action, to all of
\widetilde{W}\oplus\mathbb{Z}.

By (
21
) it follows that

A_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\mathbf{x};u)\mathbf{Z}\!\left(\mathbf{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)=A_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\mathbf{x};u)\cdot\sum_{w\,\in\,W}\overline{1|w}\!\left(\mathbf{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)\;=\sum_{w\,\in\,W}\overline{1|\tau w}(\mathbf{x};u)=\overline{Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W,F}}(\mathbf{x};u)

where
F=1|\tau,
and

Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W,F}=\sum_{w\,\in\,W}F|w
.
The function
F
is the sum of the
entries in the first column of
A_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
, so it is a
polynomial in
\mathbf{x}
. By Proposition
3.3
, it follows
that
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W,F}=aZ_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
, with
a\in\mathbb{C}\!\left(u,\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)
,
and thus the matrix
A=A_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}/\penalty a
satisfies all
the conditions in the theorem. Given
A_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
, the constant
a

can be determined explicitly using the algorithm in the
proof of Proposition
3.3
. More explicitly,
the leading term among all the entries of
A_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
is

\mathbf{x}^{16\delta}
(it occurs in the
(2,2)
entry), and the
value of
a
is given by

a=u^{\scriptscriptstyle 8}\mathbf{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle 5\delta}(\mathbf{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})^{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(\mathbf{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle\delta}-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 4})(\mathbf{x}^{\scriptscriptstyle 3\delta}-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}).\qed

We shall also need the following information about
\Lambda_{\tau^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

— The vector function

\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{x};u)
satisfies the functional equation

\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{x};u)=B(\mathbf{x};u)\mathbf{Z}\!\left(\mathbf{x};u\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)

for a 3 by 3 matrix
B(\mathbf{x};u)
satisfying, in addition,
the following two conditions:

\circ

The matrix

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\,\in\,\Phi_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}\\
\alpha\,<\,\delta\end{subarray}}\!\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{2\alpha}\right)\cdot B(\mathbf{x};u)

has polynomial entries in
\mathbf{x}
and
u.

\circ

Each entry of
B(\mathbf{x};u)
is divisible by

\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\!.

Proof.

Take

B(\mathbf{x};u)=\Lambda_{\tau^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}}(\mathbf{x};u)=A^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\!\left(\mathbf{x};u\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)
,
with
A
from Theorem
4.2
. The functional equation
fol-lows at once from the previous theorem, and the
other conditions follow from the explicit formula of
A,

see
[
1
]
.
∎

We shall also need:

Lemma 4.4
.

— The specialization
of the vector function
\mathbf{Z}
to
u=0
is given by

\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{x};0)=\,\!^{t}(\Delta(\mathbf{x}),0,0).

Proof.

By induction on length, for
w\in W,
we have

1|w(\mathbf{x};0)=(-1)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\ell(w)$}}\prod_{\beta\,\in\,\Phi(w)}\mathbf{x}^{2\beta},

where we recall that
\Phi(w)=\Phi^{+}\cap w^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(\Phi^{-}).

Thus our assertion is just Macdonald’s identity
[
40
]
in type

D_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle(1)$}}.

∎

Note that, by combining the functional equations (
18
)
and (
24
), we get

\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{x};u)=\Lambda_{w\tau^{k}}(\mathbf{x};u)\mathbf{Z}\!\left(w\mathbf{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{k\delta}\right)

(25)

for all
w\in\widetilde{W}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

5
Renormalization

The correction of the Chinta-Gunnells average for the affine root
system
D_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}
is given
by

\begin{split}\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)&=\prod_{n\,\geqslant\,1}\!\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{(2n-1)\delta}\right)^{-2}\cdot\,Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{CG}}(\mathbf{x};u)\\
&=\frac{1}{\Delta(\mathbf{x})\prod_{n\,\geqslant\,1}\!\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{(2n-1)\delta}\right)^{\!\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\,\cdot\sum_{w\,\in\,W}1|w(\mathbf{x};u).\end{split}

(26)

As we shall see in Section
8
, this function is directly
connected to a Weyl group multiple Dirichlet series associated with
the
4
-th moment of quadratic Dirichlet
L
-functions.

Letting
D(\mathbf{x};u),
the denominator of

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u),
be defined by

D(\mathbf{x};u):=\prod_{\alpha\in\Phi_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}}\!\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{2\alpha}\right)

(27)

we can now show the following:

Theorem 5.1
.

—
The function
D\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
is holomorphic in the region

|\mathbf{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\delta$}}|<1.

Proof.

For
|\mathbf{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\delta$}}|<1,
the absolutely convergent product

\Delta^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{im}$}}(\mathbf{x}):=\prod_{n\,\geqslant\,1}\!\left(1-\mathbf{x}^{2n\delta}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}

is non-vanishing, and the divisibility (in the obvious sense) of
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
by

\Delta^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}=\Delta/\penalty\Delta^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{im}$}}

in this region has already been discussed in
[
13
, Section 4]
.

On the other hand, by (
12
) and (
8
), the function

Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
is a sum of rational functions whose denominators are
products of
distinct
factors of the form
1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{2\alpha}

(
\alpha\in\Phi_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}
).
It follows that
DZ_{\scriptscriptstyle W}/\penalty\Delta
is holomorphicwhen

|\mathbf{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\delta$}}|<1,
and by Theorem
4.3
, that

(D/\penalty\Delta)\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{x};u)
is divisible by

\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\!.
Accordingly, the vector function

(D/\penalty\Delta)\mathbf{Z}\!\left(\mathbf{x};u\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)
is divisible by

\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{3\delta}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\!,
and by the functional equation

\left(\frac{D}{\Delta}\mathbf{Z}\right)\!(\mathbf{x};u)\;=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\,\in\,\Phi_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}\\
\alpha\,<\,\delta\end{subarray}}\!\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{2\alpha}\right)\cdot B(\mathbf{x};u)\left(\frac{D}{\Delta}\mathbf{Z}\right)\!\left(\mathbf{x};u\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)

so does
(D/\penalty\Delta)\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{x};u).
Proceeding by induction on
n,

we see at once that
(D/\penalty\Delta)\mathbf{Z}(\mathbf{x};u)
is divisible by
\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{(2n-1)\delta}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}

for all
n\geqslant 1.
Thus
DZ_{\scriptscriptstyle W}/\penalty\Delta
is also divisible by the product

\prod_{n\,\geqslant\,1}\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{(2n-1)\delta}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\!,

which completes the proof.
∎

Since

Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}

is
W
​–invariant under the Chinta-Gunnells action, it follows from
(
10
) and
the
W
​–invariance ofthe
product over imaginary roots that
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}

itself satisfies a functional equation with respect to each

w\in W
. More precisely, we have:

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}=\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\|w

(28)

where we write
\|
for the action defined on generators by

f\|\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}(\mathbf{x}):=-\frac{1}{x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}f|\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}f_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{-}(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\mathbf{x})+\frac{1-u/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}{1-ux_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}f_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{+}(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\mathbf{x}).

It is clear that the subspace
\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{x},u)_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
defined in
Lemma
4.1
is invariant under this action.
The functional equations satisfied by the vector
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}:=\overline{\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}}
are:

\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{\Lambda}_{w}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(w\mathbf{x};u)

(29)

where
\tilde{\Lambda}_{w}(\mathbf{x})=\tilde{\Lambda}_{w}(\mathbf{x};u)
is the 3 by 3 matrix-cocycle such that

\overline{f\|w}(\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{\Lambda}_{w}(\mathbf{x})\bar{f}(w\mathbf{x};u)

(30)

for
w\in W
and
f\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{x},u)_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
.
On generators,
\tilde{\Lambda}_{w}(\mathbf{x})
is given by

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\sigma_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}\!(\mathbf{x})=-\frac{1}{x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\Lambda_{\sigma_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}\!(\mathbf{x})
.

The following lemma provides some structural properties of the
function
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
that will
be used to get some analytic information about the Weyl
group multiple Dirichlet series we will introduce in
Section
7
.

Lemma 5.2
.

— Set

\underline{x}=(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}),

\underline{k}=(k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}})

and
l=k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}.
Then we have:

1.

The function
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
can be written as

\begin{split}\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)\,&=\,\frac{\sum_{l-\mathrm{even}}P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}{\prod_{j=1}^{4}(1-ux_{\!\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}})}\;+\sum_{l-\mathrm{odd}}P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}\\
&=\,\frac{\sum_{|\underline{k}|-\mathrm{even}}Q_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};u)\underline{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}}{1-ux_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\;\;+\sum_{|\underline{k}|-\mathrm{odd}}Q_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};u)\underline{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}\end{split}

where
P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)
and

Q_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};u)
are
polynomials in
x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},u

and
x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}},u,
respectively.
Here we set
|\underline{k}|=
k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}+\cdots+k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}.

2.

The power series obtained by expanding

\sum_{l\,\geqslant\,0}P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}

is absolutely convergent for arbitrary

\underline{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4},
provided

|x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}|
is sufficiently small, and the power series obtained by expanding

\sum_{|\underline{k}|\,\geqslant\,0}Q_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};u)\underline{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}

is absolutely convergent for any
x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\in\mathbb{C},
provided all

|x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}|,\ldots,|x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}|
are sufficiently small.

3.

We have

P_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(\underline{x};u)=Q_{\scriptscriptstyle\underline{0}}(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};u)\equiv 1

where
\underline{0}=(0,\ldots,0).

4.

The polynomials
P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)
are symmetric in

\underline{x},
and if
l
is odd then
P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)
is even, i.e.,

P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)=P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(-\underline{x};u).

5.

We have the functional equations

P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}},x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}};u)=x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{\scriptscriptstyle l-\delta_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}}P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}\bigg(\frac{1}{x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}},x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}},x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}};u\bigg)\;\;\;\;\mathrm{and}\;\;\;\;Q_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};u)=x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}^{\scriptscriptstyle|\underline{k}|-\delta_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle|\underline{k}|$}}}Q_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}\bigg(\frac{1}{x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}};u\bigg)

(31)

with
\delta_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle n$}}\!=0
or
1
according as
n
is even or odd.

Proof.

For notational simplicity, we denote

\tilde{Z}(\mathbf{x}):=\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
in this proof (the variable
u
being
fixed). The functional equation of
\tilde{Z}(\mathbf{x})
can be
broken into its even and odd parts, according to
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
, as:

\tilde{Z}^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\!(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1-u/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}{1-ux_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}\tilde{Z}^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\!(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\mathbf{x}),\quad\tilde{Z}^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\!(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}\tilde{Z}^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\!(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\mathbf{x})

for
i=1,\ldots,5
. Note that
\tilde{Z}^{\pm}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=\tilde{Z}^{\pm}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
for

i=1,\ldots,4
. The even functional equations can
be expressed in terms of the functions

G_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{4}(1-ux_{\scriptscriptstyle i})\tilde{Z}^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\!(\mathbf{x}),\quad G_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}(\mathbf{x})=(1-ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})\tilde{Z}^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\!(\mathbf{x})

as
G_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\mathbf{x})=G_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\mathbf{x})
for
i=1,\ldots,4
,
and
G_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}(\mathbf{x})=G_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\mathbf{x})
.

By Theorem
5.1
,
the function
D\tilde{Z}
is holomorphic in

\Omega:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{5}:|\mathbf{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\delta$}}|<1\},

and in this domain, it satisfies the functional equations

D\tilde{Z}^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\!(\mathbf{x})=\frac{x_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}(1+ux_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}})}{u+x_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}D\tilde{Z}^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\!(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\mathbf{x}),\quad D\tilde{Z}^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\!(\mathbf{x})=-\,\frac{x_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}(1-u^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}x_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}})}{u^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}-x_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}D\tilde{Z}^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\!(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\mathbf{x})

for
i=1,\ldots,5
, which imply that

D\tilde{Z}^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\!(\mathbf{x})

and

D\tilde{Z}^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\!(\mathbf{x})

are divisible by
1+ux_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}
and

1-u^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}x_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},

respectively. Thus the functions

F_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{+}\!(\mathbf{x})\,:=\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Phi_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}\\
\alpha\,\neq\,\alpha_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,\alpha_{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}\end{subarray}}\!\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{2\alpha}\right)\cdot G_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\mathbf{x}),\quad F_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{-}\!(\mathbf{x})\,:=\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Phi_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}\\
\alpha\,\neq\,\alpha_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,\alpha_{\scalebox{0.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}\end{subarray}}\!\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{2\alpha}\right)\cdot\tilde{Z}^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\!(\mathbf{x})

and

H_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}^{+}\!(\mathbf{x})\,:=\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Phi_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}\\
\alpha\,\neq\,\alpha_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\end{subarray}}\!\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{2\alpha}\right)\cdot G_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}(\mathbf{x}),\quad H_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}^{-}\!(\mathbf{x})\,:=\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha\in\Phi_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}\\
\alpha\,\neq\,\alpha_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\end{subarray}}\!\left(1-u^{2}\mathbf{x}^{2\alpha}\right)\cdot\tilde{Z}^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\!(\mathbf{x})

are still holomorphic in
\Omega.
Notice that, for
i=1,\ldots,4,

F_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{+}\!(\mathbf{x})
is

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
-invariant,

F_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{-}\!(\mathbf{x})=x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}F_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{-}\!(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\mathbf{x}),

and since
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
is just permuting the roots in

\Phi_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}\setminus\{\alpha_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,\alpha_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}\},

the product in the definition of

F_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{\pm}
is also
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
-invariant. By expanding the inverse of this product, and

F_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{\pm}\!(\mathbf{x})
in
power series, we can write

G_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\mathbf{x})\;=\sum_{l-\mathrm{even}}P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}},\quad\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{-}\!(\mathbf{x})\;=\sum_{l-\mathrm{odd}}P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}};

these expansions hold as long as
|\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}|<|u|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}
for all

\alpha\in\Phi_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}\setminus\{\alpha_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,\alpha_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}\}

(e.g.,
\underline{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
is arbitrary,
and
|x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}|
is sufficiently small),
which justifies the first part (i.e., the
P
-part) of
2
. The functional
equation (
31
) of the coefficients
P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)
now follows from the
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
-invariance
of
G_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and the functional equation

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{-}\!(\mathbf{x})=x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{-}\!(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\mathbf{x}).

It follows that

P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)

are polynomials in

x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},u,

and our assertions
4
follow from the fact that

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{-}\!(\mathbf{x})=\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{-}\!(\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\mathbf{x})
(as
\tilde{Z}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{o}$}}=0
),
and from the symmetry of

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{+}\!(\mathbf{x})

and

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{-}\!(\mathbf{x})

with respect to
x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}.

The
Q
-parts of
1, 2
and
5
follow from the same argument, applied
to
H_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{+}
and
H_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{-}
.

Finally, notice that

\tilde{Z}(\underline{x},0)=\Delta(\underline{x},0)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}\,\cdot\sum_{w\,\in\,\langle\sigma_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}\rangle_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1\leqslant i\leqslant 4$}}}1|w(\underline{x},0)=\prod_{j=1}^{4}(1-ux_{\!\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}})^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}

and

\tilde{Z}(\underline{0},x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}})=\Delta(\underline{0},x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}})^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(1+1|\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}))=(1-ux_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}})^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}

which give
3
. This completes the proof.
∎

The extra functional equation satisfied by
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)
is

\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u)\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}\!\left(\mathbf{x};u\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)

(32)

where
\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u)=B(\mathbf{x};u)/\penalty\big(1-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}x^{\delta}\big)^{\scriptscriptstyle 2},

with the matrix
B(\mathbf{x};u)
from Theorem
4.3
. Using
this functional equation, we now show that
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)
is
completely determined by
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};0)
and
\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u).

By contrast, thefunctional equations (
29
) alone determine
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)
only up to a power
series in
u
and
\mathbf{x}^{\delta}
(see
[
13
, Theo-rem 3.7]
).

Lemma 5.3
.

—
Expand the vector function
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)
as

\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)=\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}(\mathbf{x})+u\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}(\mathbf{x})+u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}(\mathbf{x})+\cdots

and let

\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{B}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}(\mathbf{x})+u\tilde{B}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}(\mathbf{x})+\cdots
. Then we have

\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}(\mathbf{x})=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad\tilde{B}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}(\mathbf{x})=\tilde{B}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}\!:=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

and the matrix
\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u)
determines
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)

recursively by

\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})=(I-\mathbf{x}^{n\delta}\tilde{B}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}})^{-1}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathbf{x}^{i\delta}\tilde{B}_{n-i}(\mathbf{x})\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{i}(\mathbf{x})

(33)

for
n\geqslant 1
, where
I
is the identity matrix.

Proof.

The expression for

\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}(\mathbf{x})

follows at once from Lemma
4.4
, and the
explicit formula of the matrix
B(\mathbf{x};u)
yields

\tilde{B}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}.

The recursion of
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{n}(\mathbf{x})
follows at once from (
32
).
∎

The following corollary will be needed in the proof of the main
theorem in Section
8
.

Corollary 5.4
.

— We have that

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)\!\!\!\pmod{u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}=1+u(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}+x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}+x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}+x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}+x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}).

Proof.

From the explicit formula of the matrix
\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u)
, one
finds that

\tilde{B}_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}(\mathbf{x})=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{x}^{\delta}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}&\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\!\left(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}+x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}+x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}+x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}\right)\\
x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}&0&0\\
x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}+x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}+x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}+x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.

The formula now follows from (
33
) with
n=1
,
after multiplying by the row vector
(1,1,1).

∎

We shall also need a positivity result about the specializations

P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{1};u)
of the polynomials
in Lemma
5.2
, with
\underline{1}=(1,1,1,1)
.

Corollary 5.5
.

— The polynomial

P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{1};u)
for
l
odd,
and the power series

P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{1};u)/\penalty(1-u)^{4}

for
l
even have non-negative coefficients.

Proof.

By Proposition
A.2
, the entries of the
matrix
\tilde{B}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};u)
, as power series in

x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
and
u
, have non-negative
coefficients. ​Thus the matrices
\tilde{B}_{n}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})

have entries with non-negative coefficients, and by
in-duction using (
33
), the same is true for the vectors
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{n}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})
.
​Accordingly, the function

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};u)

has non-negative coefficients when expanded as a power series,
and now Lemma
5.2
finishes the proof.
∎

6
Residues

By Theorem
5.1
, the
singularities of the function
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)

can only occur at the zeros of the denominator
D(\mathbf{x};u),
and
thus
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
can only have
simple poles. Our goal is now to compute the residue of this function at each of
these poles.

The following theorem provides the key calculation:

Theorem 6.1
.

— The residue of

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
at

x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}=u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}

is given by the formula

\begin{split}R(\underline{x};u):&=\lim_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\to\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty$}u}(1-ux_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}})\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)\\
&=\frac{1}{\left(P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\!\left(u_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\prod_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i=1$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}\left(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\!\left(u_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-2$}}P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\cdot\;\,\prod_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1\,\leqslant\,i\,<\,j\,\leqslant\,4$}}\left(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}};P\right)_{\infty}}\end{split}

where

P=(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}})/\penalty u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}

and
(a;b)_{\infty}=\prod_{k\geqslant 0}\,(1-ab^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle k$}})
is the

b
-Pochhammer symbol.

The proof of this theorem will be given in the rest of this section.
Using results in
[
27
]
, similar ideas can beused to prove
explicit formulas for multiple residues of the average zeta
function of arbitrary simply laced reduced irreducible affine root
systems.

To compute the residue at any other pole of

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u),
let
\alpha
be an
affine positive real root, and write it as
\alpha=\sum_{i}n_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i}

in terms of simple roots. Let

w_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}

be an element of the Weyl group sending
\alpha
to

\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5},
and if
\alpha\neq\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i},
hence
n_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\geqslant 1,
let
\zeta
be a

2n_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
-th root of
1
in
\mathbb{C}.

Let

C_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u)

denote the limit

C_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u):=\hskip-44.0pt\lim_{\hskip 44.0ptx_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\to\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.75}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}}\!\left(1-\zeta u^{1/\penalty n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha/\penalty n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\right)\!\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)

where

\alpha^{\prime}:=\alpha-n_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
.
Here, we chose the principal branch of the complex logarithm, and so

C_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u)

is well-defined and analytic at least
when
x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}
and
u
are away from the
non-positive real axis.

Lemma 6.2
.

— With notation as above, we have

C_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u)=\frac{1}{2n_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\!\left.\left\{R(w_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\underline{x};u)\cdot f_{w_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}(\mathbf{x};u)\right\}\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.75}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}}

(34)

where
f_{w}=(1+ux_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}})\|w
and

w_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\underline{x}

represents the first four components of

w_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\mathbf{x}.

Proof.

By applying the functional equation (
29
) of the vector function
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}=\overline{\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}}
corresponding to

w=\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 5},
one finds that

\lim_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\to e\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle/$}u}(1-eux_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}})\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)=\frac{1}{2}R(\underline{x};u)\cdot\,\!^{t}(1,e,0)

where
e\in\{-1,1\}
. Applying (
29
) again, for

w=w_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}},

we obtain

\hskip-44.0pt\lim_{\hskip 44.0ptx_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\to\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.75}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}}\!\left(1-\zeta u^{1/\penalty n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha/\penalty n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\right)\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)=\frac{1}{2n_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\!\left.\left\{R(w_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\underline{x};u)\tilde{\Lambda}_{w_{\alpha}}\!(\mathbf{x})\right\}\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.75}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}}\cdot\,\!^{t}(1,\zeta^{n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\!,0).

The formula in the lemma now follows using (
30
),
after multiplying on the left by the vector
(1,1,1)
.
∎

If
\alpha=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i},
for some
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4,

the residue of
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
at

x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}=1/\penalty u
can be
computed similarly from the functional equation (
29
)
with

w_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}=\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 5},

which sends the simple root
\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
to

\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}.

6.1
Proof of Theorem
6.1

We start by recalling a result from
[
13
]
, which identifies the
residue
R(\underline{x};u)
up to a function depending only upon

P=(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}})/\penalty u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}

and
u
.

Proposition 6.3
.

— We have

R(\underline{x};u)=f(P,u)\cdot\frac{1}{\prod_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i=1$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}\left(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\!\left(u_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-2$}}P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\cdot\;\,\prod_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1\,\leqslant\,i\,<\,j\,\leqslant\,4$}}\left(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}};P\right)_{\infty}}

where the function
f(P,u)
is meromorphic in the region
|P|<1
,
with possible poles only when
P^{n}=\pm u^{\scriptscriptstyle-2}

forsome
n\geqslant 1
. In addition, if we set

\mathrm{z}:=x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}
,
then for every
\epsilon>0
, the function

f(u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\mathrm{z},u)

is holomorphic inthe polydisc

|\mathrm{z}|<(1+\epsilon)^{\scriptscriptstyle-4},\,|u|<1+\epsilon.

Proof.

For
|P|
sufficiently small, the decomposition follows
from
[
13
, Lemma 4.30]
. From
Theorem
5.1
, itfollows that the function

\left.\left[D(\mathbf{x};u)/\penalty(1-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\right]\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}=\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty$}u}\cdot R(\underline{x};u)

is holomorphic in the region
|P|<1
,
u\neq 0
; when
|P|
is small,
we can express this function as

f(P,u)\left(P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\!\left(u_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\prod_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i=1$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}\left(u_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\!\left(u_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-2$}}P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\,\cdot\,\prod_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1\,\leqslant\,i\,<\,j\,\leqslant\,4$}}\left(-x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}};P\right)_{\infty}.

However, the product of the Pochhammer symbols is holomorphic when

|P|<1
, and if in addition we take
P^{n}\neq\pm u^{\scriptscriptstyle-2}
for all
n\geqslant 1
, we can choose
x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in\mathbb{C}

(i=1,\ldots,4)

such that this product is non-zero. It follows that indeed

f(P,u)
is holomorphic for
|P|<1
except for possible
poles when
P^{n}=\pm u^{\scriptscriptstyle-2}
for some
n\geqslant 1
,
and that the decomposition of
R(\underline{x};u)
extends by
analytic continuation.

On the other hand, by Lemma
5.2
,
part
1
, and the functional equation
(
31
) of the polynomials

Q_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};u)
,
we have

R(u\underline{x};u)\;=\sum_{|\underline{k}|\,\equiv\,0\!\!\!\!\pmod{2}}Q_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}(u;u)\underline{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}

(35)

which implies easily that

f(u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\mathrm{z},u)

(as a function of the variables

\mathrm{z}=x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}

and
u
) is holomorphic in the polydisc

|\mathrm{z}|<(1+\epsilon)^{\scriptscriptstyle-4},\,|u|<1+\epsilon.

This completes the proof.
∎

Thus it remains to show that

f(P,u)=\frac{1}{\left(P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\!\left(u_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}}.

(36)

The proof of this formula involves the following two steps:

(1)

Formula (
36
) holds for
u=-1
. This is an immediate consequence of Macdonald’s formula.

(2)

The function

g(P,u)=f(P,u)\left(P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}\!\left(u_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}

is invariant under the transformation
(P,u)\mapsto(P,u/\penalty P).

(It is here where we crucially use the extrafunctional equation
from
4.2
.)

The proofs of (1) and (2) will be given in the next two subsections,
but for now let us show how they imply(
36
).

By Proposition
6.3
, the function

g(u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\mathrm{z},u)

is holomorphic in the polydisc

|\mathrm{z}|<(1+\epsilon)^{\scriptscriptstyle-4},\,|u|<1+\epsilon
,
hencea normally convergent power series
in
\mathrm{z}
and
u
. Substituting

\mathrm{z}\to P/\penalty u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}
,
we can then write

g(P,u)\;=\sum_{m,n\in\mathbb{Z}}a_{m,n}u^{m}P^{n}

the series being normally convergent for, say

\epsilon\leqslant|u|<1+\epsilon

and

|P|<|u|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}(1+\epsilon)^{\scriptscriptstyle-4}
.
Notice that
a_{m,n}=0
if
n<0
. Since

g(P,u)=g(P,uP)
, it follows at once (after
taking
u
and
P
such that
\epsilon\leqslant|uP|<1+\epsilon
)
that
a_{m,n}=a_{m,n-m}
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Iterating, one finds that

a_{m,n}=a_{m,n+km}

for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
a_{m,n}=0
for
n<0
, it
follows that
a_{m,n}=0
if
m\neq 0
. Therefore

g(P,u)
is independent of
u
, and by (1),
g\equiv 1
.

6.2
The residue for
u=-1

From the relation between
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
and
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
, we can write

R(\underline{x};u)=\frac{1}{\left(u_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}P_{\phantom{i}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\cdot\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}(\underline{x},1/\penalty u)}\,\cdot\lim_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\to\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty$}u}\frac{1-ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}}{1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)

where we put

\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}(\mathbf{x}):=\Delta(\mathbf{x})/\penalty(1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
.
When
u=-1
, we see by induction on
\ell(w)
that

1|w(\mathbf{x};-1)=(-1)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\ell(w)$}}\prod_{\beta\,\in\,\Phi(w)}\mathbf{x}^{\beta}

and so
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};-1)=F_{\scriptscriptstyle MD}(\mathbf{x})
, where

F_{\scriptscriptstyle MD}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{w\in W}(-1)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\ell(w)$}}\prod_{\beta\,\in\,\Phi(w)}\mathbf{x}^{\beta}

is the function studied by Macdonald
[
40
]
. Then

\lim_{u\to-1}\,\lim_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\to\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty$}u}\frac{1-ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}}{1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)=\frac{1}{2}Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\underline{x},-1;-1)=\frac{1}{2}F_{\scriptscriptstyle MD}(\underline{x},-1)

where in the first equality we interchanged the two limits;
this is justified since
\Delta(\mathbf{x})
, and in particular

1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, divides
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)

(see
[
13
, Section 4]
), and so the function inside the
double limit is continuous at
(1,-1)
as a function of

z=ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
and
u
. Consequently,

R(\underline{x};-1)=\frac{\frac{1}{2}F_{\scriptscriptstyle MD}(\underline{x},-1)}{\left(P_{\phantom{i}};P_{\phantom{i}}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\right)_{\infty}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}\cdot\Delta_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}(\underline{x},-1)}.

(37)

Using the list of roots of
D_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
at the beginning
of Section
4
, we have by Macdonald’s formula
[
40
]

\displaystyle F_{\scriptscriptstyle MD}(\mathbf{x})=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{4}\left(x_{\scriptscriptstyle i};\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)_{\infty}\left(x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\mathbf{x}^{\delta};\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)_{\infty}\left(x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)_{\infty}\left(x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\mathbf{x}^{\delta};\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)_{\infty}\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 4}\left(x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}x_{\scriptscriptstyle j}x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)_{\infty}

\displaystyle\cdot\left(\mathbf{x}^{\delta};\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}_{\infty}\left(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)_{\infty}\left(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\mathbf{x}^{\delta};\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)_{\infty}.

Also,
\Delta(\mathbf{x})=F_{\scriptscriptstyle MD}(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2},\ldots,x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
,
and one can easily check that (
37
) matches the formula
in Theorem
6.1
for
u=-1
.
This completes the proof of step (1) in
6.1
.

6.3
Functional equations of the residue function

From the formula of the residue function in Proposition

6.3
, it is clear that

R(\underline{x};u)=R(-\underline{x};u).

Then by Lemma
5.2
, part
​
1
, it follows at once that

\lim_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\to\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty$}u}(1-ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)=\frac{1}{2}R(\underline{x};u)\cdot v_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}

(38)

where we set
v_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}=\,\!^{t}(1,1,0)
.

We need the extension of the cocycle
\tilde{\Lambda}(\mathbf{x};u)
to
W\oplus\mathbb{Z}
,
as in Section
4.2
, by

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-1}}(\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u)
,
with
\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u)
defined as in the line following (
32
), and

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau}(\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x};u):=A(\mathbf{x};u)\cdot\left(1-u^{2}/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\right)^{2}

with
A(\mathbf{x};u)
from Theorem
4.2
. ​Then the functional
equation (
25
) holds for
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)
,
with
\Lambda(\mathbf{x};u)
re-placed by
\tilde{\Lambda}(\mathbf{x};u)
.
As in Section
4.2
, we have an action of
\tau
extending the action
\|

such that

\overline{f\|\tau}(\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x};u)\cdot\bar{f}(\mathbf{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta})

(39)

for all
f\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{x},u)_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
.

Proposition 6.4
.

— Let
w\in W
be such that

w\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}-\delta
. Then

R(\underline{x};u)=\lambda_{w}(\underline{x};u)\cdot\!\left.R(w\underline{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta})\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}=\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty u$}}

where

\lambda_{w}(\underline{x};u)=\left.[(1+ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})\|\tau w]^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}(\mathbf{x};u)\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}=\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty u$}}
.

Notice from (
39
) that

[(1+ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})\|\tau](\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{A}_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}(\mathbf{x};u)+(ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta})\tilde{A}_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}(\mathbf{x};u)
,
where
\tilde{A}_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}
(resp. ​
\tilde{A}_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}
) is thesum of the entries
in the first (resp. ​second) column of the matrix
\tilde{A}(\mathbf{x};u)
.

Proof.

Since

w\mathbf{x}=(w\underline{x},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\mathbf{x}^{\delta})
,
the analogue of the functional equation (
25
) for

\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u),
applied for
w\tau
, together with (
38
) gives

R(\underline{x};u)v_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}=\!\left.\left\{R(w\underline{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta})\tilde{\Lambda}_{w\tau}(\mathbf{x};u)\right\}\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}=\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty u$}}\cdot\,v_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}.

Note that

\tilde{\Lambda}_{w\tau}(\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau}(\mathbf{x};u)\tilde{\Lambda}_{w}(\mathbf{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta})

does not have a pole at
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}=1/\penalty u;
by Theorem
4.2
,
the ma-trix
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau}(\mathbf{x};u)
does not have a pole there,
and since
\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}+\delta\notin\Phi(w)
, neither does

\tilde{\Lambda}_{w}(\mathbf{x};u/\penalty\mathbf{x}^{\delta})
. We thus ob-tain the functional
equation of
R(\underline{x};u)
, with
\lambda_{w}(\underline{x};u)
such that

\left.\tilde{\Lambda}_{w\tau}(\mathbf{x};u)\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}=\scalebox{1.15}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty u$}}\cdot\,v_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}=\lambda_{w}(\underline{x};u)v_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}.

Com-bining (
39
) and (
30
), we get the formula for

\lambda_{w}(\underline{x};u)
, and completes the proof.
∎

By applying this proposition to the element

t=\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
,
we obtain that

R(\underline{x};u)=\lambda_{t}(\underline{x};u)R\left(u/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,u/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4};u/\penalty P\right)

with
\lambda_{t}(\underline{x};u)
computed explicitly from the matrix

A(\mathbf{x};u)
in
[
1
]
as

\lambda_{t}(\underline{x};u)=(1-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\prod_{i=1}^{4}(1-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 4}(1-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}x_{\scriptscriptstyle j}).

One can check easily that this factor matches the one from the same
functional equation applied to the right-hand side of the formula in
Theorem
6.1
, and thus completing the proof
of step (2) in
6.1
.

7
WMDS associated to moments of L-series

By Theorem
5.1
and the definition (
27
) of

D(\mathbf{x};u),
when
0<u<1,
the function
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
is holomorphic for

|x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}|<1
(
i=1,\ldots,5
), and thus, in this polydisc,

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)=1+\sum_{\mathrm{k}\,\neq\,\mathbf{0}}a(\mathrm{k};u)\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{k}}

(40)

the sum being over tuples

\mathrm{k}=(k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}})\in\mathbb{N}^{5}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}.

The coefficients
a(\mathrm{k};u)
are polynomials in
u,
and by Cauchy’s inequalities,
for every
\epsilon>0,
we have the estimate

|a(\mathrm{k};u)|\ll_{\epsilon}u^{-\epsilon|\mathrm{k}|}.

Before making the transition to multiple Dirichlet series,
let us briefly recall some facts about quadratic Dirichlet

L
-functions in the rational function field setting.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{\!q}
be a finite field of odd characteristic.
For

m\in\mathbb{F}_{\!q}[x],

m\neq 0,
set

|m|=q^{\deg m},

and for
d,m\in\mathbb{F}_{\!q}[x],

d\neq 0
and

m
monic, let
\chi_{d}(m)=(d/\penalty m)
denote
the quadratic symbol. We define
\chi_{d}(1)=1,
and if

d\in\mathbb{F}_{\!q}^{\times},
we have
\chi_{d}(m)=\mathrm{sgn}(d)^{\deg m},

for all non-constant
m\in\mathbb{F}_{\!q}[x],

where
\mathrm{sgn}(d)=1
or
-1
according as

d\in(\mathbb{F}_{\!q}^{\times})^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}

or not.

For
d
square-free, the
L
-function associated
to the primitive character
\chi_{d}
is defined by

L(s,\chi_{d})\;\;=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\,\in\,\mathbb{F}_{\!q}[x]\\
m-\text{monic}\end{subarray}}\chi_{d}(m)|m|^{-s}\;\;\,=\prod_{p-\text{monic \& irreducible}}\big(1-\chi_{d}(p)|p|^{-s}\big)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}

for complex
s,
with
\Re(s)>1.
When
d

is non-constant this
L
-function turns out to be a polynomial
in
q^{-s}
of degree
\deg d-1,
and if

d\in\mathbb{F}_{\!q}^{\times},

L(s,\chi_{d})=\frac{1}{1-\mathrm{sgn}(d)q^{{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}-s}};

when

d\in(\mathbb{F}_{\!q}^{\times})^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},

the
L
-function is just
\zeta(s)
–the zeta-function. In addition,
if one defines

\gamma_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}(s,d)

by

\gamma_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}(s,d)=q^{\frac{1}{2}{\scalebox{1.05}{$\scriptscriptstyle\left(3\,+\,(-1)^{\deg d}\right)$}}\left(s-\frac{1}{2}\right)}\big(1-\mathrm{sgn}(d)q^{-s}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\left(1\,+\,(-1)^{\deg d}\right)$}}\big(1-\mathrm{sgn}(d)q^{s-\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\left(1\,+\,(-1)^{\deg d}\right)$}}

then
L(s,\chi_{d})
satisfies the functional equation

L(s,\chi_{d})=\gamma_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}(s,d)|d|^{\frac{1}{2}-s}L(1-s,\chi_{d}).

We now use the coefficients
a(\mathrm{k};u)
to build the relevant
family of multiple Dirichlet series.

Let
\mathbb{F}_{\!q}
be a finite field of odd characteristic, and fix an element

\theta_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}\in\mathbb{F}_{\!q}^{\times}\setminus(\mathbb{F}_{\!q}^{\times})^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}.

For

\mathbf{s}=(s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,s_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})

and

a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\in\{1,\theta_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}\},

we define

\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})

by a series (summed over monics in
\mathbb{F}_{\!q}[x]
) of the form

\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})\;\;\;=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},\,d-\mathrm{monic}\\
d=d_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}d_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},\;d_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}\;\mathrm{square\;free}\end{subarray}}\frac{\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}(\widehat{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\widehat{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\widehat{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\widehat{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle 4})\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}(d_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})A(m_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},m_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},m_{\scriptscriptstyle 3},m_{\scriptscriptstyle 4},d)}{|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}|d|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}}

(41)

where
\widehat{m}_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
(
i=1,\ldots,4
) is the part of

m_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
coprime to
d_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}.

The coefficients

A(m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},d)

are com-pletely determined by the following two conditions:

(i)

If
p
is monic irreducible, then

A\big(p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\!,\,p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\!,\,p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}}\!,\,p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}\!,\,p^{l}\big)=a\big(k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}},k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},l;q^{-(\deg p)/\penalty 2}\big).

(ii)

For monic

m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},d,

we have

A(m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},d)\;=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}\parallel\,m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}\\
p^{l}\parallel\,d\end{subarray}}A\big(p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\!,\,p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\!,\,p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}}\!,\,p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}\!,\,p^{l}\big)

the product being taken over monic irreducibles.

Lemma 7.1
.

— The series defining

\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})
is absolutely convergent for

\Re(s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})>1,

i=1,\ldots,5.

Proof.

The proof is similar to that of
[
23
, Lemma
6.5]
. We first show that the series (
41
) is
absolutely con-vergent when
\Re(s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})
is sufficiently
large. To see this,
by condition (i) and the estimate of the coefficients

a(\mathrm{k};u),
we have

\big|A\big(p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\!,\,p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\!,\,p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}}\!,\,p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}\!,\,p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\big)\big|\ll_{\epsilon,q}|p|^{\epsilon|\mathrm{k}|}

for every monic irreducible
p,
the implied constant depending upon

q
and
\epsilon,
but it is independent of the degree of
p.

Choosing
n\geqslant 1
such that this constant is smaller than
q^{n},

it follows from (ii) that

|A(m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},d)|<|m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}d|^{n+\epsilon}

for all monic polynomials

m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}},m_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},d.

Since

|\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})|\;\;\leqslant\sum_{m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},\,d}\,\frac{|A(m_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},m_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},m_{\scriptscriptstyle 3},m_{\scriptscriptstyle 4},d)|}{|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Re(s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}})$}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Re(s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}})$}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Re(s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}})$}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Re(s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}})$}}|d|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Re(s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}})$}}}

we have absolute convergence as long as

\Re(s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})>n+1+\epsilon.
On the other hand,
the right-hand side of the last inequality decomposes as

\prod_{p}\left(1+\sum_{\mathrm{k}\neq\mathbf{0}}\big|a\big(\mathrm{k};|p|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2$}}\big)\big||p|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-\langle\mathrm{k},\Re({\mathbf{s}})\rangle$}}\right)

where

\Re({\mathbf{s}}):=(\Re(s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}),\ldots,\Re(s_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})),

and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the scalar product on

\mathbb{R}^{5}.
Taking the logarithm, our asser-tion now
follows by a simple comparison with the series

\sum_{p}\sum_{\mathrm{k}\neq\mathbf{0}}|p|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-\lambda|\mathrm{k}|$}}

which is easily seen to be convergent when
\lambda>1.

∎

For simplicity, we shall assume from now on that
q\equiv 1\!\pmod{4}.
Following
[
23
, Section 3]
, we can
write

\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})\;=\sum_{d=d_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}d_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{4}L\!\left(s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}+\tfrac{1}{2},\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}\right)\cdot\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\!(d_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\mathbf{s}\scalebox{1.}{${}^{\prime}$};\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})}{|d|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}}

(42)

where

P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\mathbf{s}\scalebox{1.}{${}^{\prime}$};\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})

(
\mathbf{s}\scalebox{1.}{${}^{\prime}$}:=(s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,s_{\scriptscriptstyle 4})
)
is the Dirichlet polynomial defined by

\begin{split}P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,s_{\scriptscriptstyle 4};\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})\;\;=\!&\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{l}\parallel\,d\\
l\,\equiv\,1\!\!\!\!\!\pmod{2}\end{subarray}}\,P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}\!\left(|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}},\ldots,|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}};q^{-(\deg p)/\penalty 2}\right)\\
&\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\,\mid\,d_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}\\
p^{l}\parallel\,d\\
l\,\equiv\,0\!\!\!\!\pmod{2}\end{subarray}}\,P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}\!\left(\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}(p)|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}},\ldots,\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}(p)|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}};q^{-(\deg p)/\penalty 2}\right).\end{split}

(43)

Here
P_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle l$}}(\underline{x};u)
are the polynomials in Lemma
5.2
. The functions

P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\mathbf{s}\scalebox{1.}{${}^{\prime}$};\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})

are symmetric in all vari-ables, and by (
31
), they satisfy a functional equation as
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\to-s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}
for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4.
It is clear that (
42
)
converges absolutely for arbitrary

s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,s_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{1/\penalty 2\}

as long as
s_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
has sufficiently large real
part.

We can also write

\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})\;\;=\sum_{m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}\,=\,n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\frac{L\!\left(s_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}+\tfrac{1}{2},\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}n_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}\right)\!\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\!(n_{\scriptscriptstyle 0})Q_{\underline{m}}(s_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}n_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})}{|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}}

(44)

where, for

\underline{m}=(m_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,m_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}),

the Dirichlet polynomial

Q_{\underline{m}}(s_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}n_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})

is given by

Q_{\underline{m}}(s_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}n_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})\;\;\,=\!\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}\parallel\,m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}\\
|\underline{k}|\,\equiv\,1\!\!\!\!\!\pmod{2}\end{subarray}}Q_{\underline{k}}\!\left(|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}};q^{-(\deg p)/\penalty 2}\right)\;\;\;\cdot\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\,\mid\,n_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}\\
p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}\parallel\,m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}\\
|\underline{k}|\,\equiv\,0\!\!\!\!\pmod{2}\end{subarray}}Q_{\underline{k}}\!\left(\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}n_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}(p)|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}};q^{-(\deg p)/\penalty 2}\right).

Again, by (
31
), the polynomials

Q_{\underline{m}}(s_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}n_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})

satisfy a functional equation as
s_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\to-s_{\scriptscriptstyle 5},
and for every

s_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{1/\penalty 2\},

the series (
44
) converges absolutely as long as all

s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,s_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
have
sufficiently large real parts.

Proposition 7.2
.

— Let
\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})

denote the function
\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};1,1)
after substituting

x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}=q^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}},

i=1,\ldots,5
. Then the functions
\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})
and

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})

satisfy the same functional equation (
28
).

Proof.

This follows at once from (
31
)
and the functional equation of
L(s,\chi_{d}).

∎

Remark 7.1
.

This functional equation was stated first in
[
13
]
, in terms
of the vector function

\boldsymbol{\mathscr{W}}\!(\mathbf{s})=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};1,1)+\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};\theta_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}},1)\\
\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};1,1)-\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};\theta_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}},1)\\
\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};1,\theta_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}})+\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};\theta_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}},\theta_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}})\\
\end{pmatrix}

and predated the Chinta-Gunnells action. To make the
comparison with early work easier, and because itgeneralizes
more readily to number fields (for which the Chinta-Gunnells action
is not available), we also state the vector form
of (
28
). The vector function obtained by substituting

x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}=q^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}

(
i=1,\ldots,5
) in
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{W}}\!(\mathbf{s})

satisfies the same functional equation as the vector

\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\!(\mathbf{x};u):=\begin{pmatrix}(\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W})^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\mathbf{x};u)\\
(\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W})^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\mathbf{x};u)\\
(\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W})^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\mathbf{x};u)\\
\end{pmatrix}=V\overline{\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}}(\mathbf{x};u),\;\;\text{where }V=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&1\\
0&1&0\\
1&0&\!\!\!-1\end{pmatrix}

namely
\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\!(\mathbf{x};u)=\mathrm{M}_{w}(\mathbf{x};u)\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\!(w\mathbf{x};u)
for all
w\in W,

for the
1
-cocycle
\mathrm{M}_{w}(\mathbf{x};u)
defined on
generators by

\mathrm{M}_{\sigma_{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}\!(\mathbf{x};u)=-\frac{1}{x_{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}V\Lambda_{\sigma_{\scalebox{0.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}\!(\mathbf{x};u)V^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
.

Remark 7.2
.

​Expressions similar to (
42
), (
44
), and the conclusions of Lemma
7.1
, and Proposition
7.2
all still hold if instead of

\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})
,
one takes the multiple Dirichlet series constructed in the same way using the coefficients of

Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{CG}}(\mathbf{x};u)
. ​More generally, one can
start with any power series
F(z,u)
, and take the multiple Dirichlet series whose
p
-parts are

F\big(|p|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-\delta(\mathbf{s})$}},|p|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2$}}\big)\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}\big(|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}},\ldots,|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}};|p|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2$}}\big)
, where

\delta(\mathbf{s})=s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}+s_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+s_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}+s_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}+2s_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
.
It is easy to see that this multiple Dirichlet series is, in fact,

\prod_{p}F\big(|p|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-\delta(\mathbf{s})$}},|p|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2$}}\big)\cdot\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}).

(45)

Choosing
F(z,u)
so that the product converges absolutely when
\Re(\delta(\mathbf{s}))>6
, one sees that the multiple Dirichlet series can be expressed as in (
42
) and (
44
) for
\Re(s_{\scriptscriptstyle i})>1
,
i=1,\ldots,5
, and that it satisfies the required functional equations. Thus to determine a canonical normalization of
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
(or equivalently
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{CG}}(\mathbf{x};u)
), which eventually allows us to establish the analytic properties of the corresponding multiple Dirichlet series, some additional conditions must be imposed.

There is yet another multiple Dirichlet series

Z(\mathbf{x};q)=\sum_{\mathrm{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{r\scalebox{.93}{$\scriptscriptstyle+1$}}}b(\mathrm{k};q)\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{k}}

associated with moments of
L
-functions that is worth considering; as
before,
x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}
stands for

q^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}},

i=1,\ldots,

r+1,
and the coefficients
b(\mathrm{k};q)
are finite sums

\sum_{\lambda}c_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda$}}\lambda

over
q
-Weil algebraic integers of weights

\nu_{\scalebox{1.05}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda$}}\in\mathbb{N},

subject to the following three conditions:

(a)

Each
q
-Weil integer
\lambda
occurs in the sum together with
all its complex conjugates.

(b)

The coefficients

c_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda$}}

are rational numbers, and if
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}
are conjugates over

\mathbb{Q},
then

c_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda$}}\!=c_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda\scalebox{.75}{${}^{\prime}$}$}}.

(c)

For
|\mathrm{k}|=k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}+\cdots+k_{r\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle+1$}}>1,
we have
that

|\mathrm{k}|+2\leqslant\nu_{\scalebox{1.05}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda$}}\leqslant 2|\mathrm{k}|

for all
\lambda
occurring in the sum.

The lower bound of
\nu_{\scalebox{1.05}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda$}}
in the last condition will be referred to as

dominance.
Let
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\!q}

be a fixed algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{\!q}.
We require that
the multiple Dirichlet series to be of the form (
41
), with multiplicative coeffi-cients
B(m_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,m_{\scalebox{1.21}{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}},d),
such that the following
conditions are also satisfied:

(A1)

The sub-series

\sum_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}b(\underline{k},0;q)\underline{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{1}{1-qx_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}

where, as before,

\underline{x}:=(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,x_{\scalebox{1.3}{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}})

and

\underline{k}:=(k_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,k_{\scalebox{1.3}{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}).

In addition,

\sum_{\underline{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{r}}b(\underline{k},1;q)\underline{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}=q\;\;\;\mathrm{and}\;\;\,\sum_{l\,\geqslant\,0}b(\underline{0},l;q)x_{r\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle+1$}}^{l}\!=\frac{1}{1-qx_{r\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle+1$}}}.

In particular,
B(1,\ldots,1)=b(0,\ldots,0;q)=1.

(A2)

The coefficients
b(\mathrm{k};q^{n})
corresponding to

Z(\mathbf{x};q^{n})
over any finite field extension
\mathbb{F}_{\!q^{n}}\subset\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{\!q}
of
\mathbb{F}_{\!q}
aregiven by

b(\mathrm{k};q^{n})=\sum_{\lambda}c_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda$}}\lambda^{\!n}.

(A3)

For every monic irreducible
p\in\mathbb{F}_{\!q}[x]

of degree
e\geqslant 1,
the coefficients

B\big(p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\!,\ldots,p^{k_{\scalebox{.88}{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle+1$}}}\big)

are given by

B\big(p^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\!,\ldots,p^{k_{\scalebox{.88}{$\scriptscriptstyle r$}\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle+1$}}}\big)=q^{e|\mathrm{k}|}\!\sum_{\lambda}c_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda$}}\lambda^{\!-e}.

In
[
25
]
, the first two authors established the existence of a
unique multiple Dirichlet series satisfying all these
conditions. This axiomatic construction was generalized by Whitehead
[
49
]
to simply laced affine root systems, and by Sawin
[
47
]
, using geometric methods, in a more general setting.

One should notice that it is not a priori clear that this multiple
Dirichlet series satisfies a group of func-tional equations.
To see that this is indeed the case, one can adapt the proof of

[
49
, Proposition 2.2.1]
. However, in the next section,
this will be clarified when
r=4.

8
Comparison

Let notations
be as above. Our goal in this section is to compare the functions

\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})
and
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q}),
anddeduce from this comparison (combined
with Theorem
5.1
) the
meromorphic continuation of
\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})
to
themaximal possible region

|\mathbf{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\delta$}}|<1.

One checks that the functions
\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})
and

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})
satisfy the following conditions:

(i)

Both
\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})
and
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})
are
holomorphic for
|x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}|<1/\penalty q
(
i=1,\ldots,5
).

(ii)

Both

D(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})

and

D(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})
are power series
that converge absolutely if either
