\underline{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
and

|x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}|
is sufficiently small, or

x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\in\mathbb{C}
and

|x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}|,\ldots,|x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}|

are sufficiently small.

(iii)

Both
\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})
and
\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})

are symmetric in

x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}},

and satisfy the same functional equation(
28
).

It follows from
[
13
, Theorem 3.7]
that

\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})=C(\mathbf{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\delta$}})\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})

(46)

for some function
C
of one complex variable.

Theorem 8.1
.

— We have the equalities

\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})=\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})=Z(q^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2$}}\mathbf{x};q).

Proof.

We first show that
\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})
and

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})
have the same
residue at
x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}=1/\penalty\sqrt{q}
(hence

C(\mathbf{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\delta$}})

in (
46
)is identically
1
).

From the expression (
44
) of

\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2},a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}),

we see that this function has a (simple) pole at

s_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}=\frac{1}{2}
only if

a_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}=1,
and the part contributing to this pole is

\zeta\left(s_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\;\;\cdot\sum_{m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}m_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}\,=\,\square}\;\frac{Q_{\underline{m}}(s_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};1)}{|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}}|m_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}|^{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}}\,=\,\zeta\left(s_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\right)\,\cdot\,\prod_{p}\bigg(\sum_{|\underline{k}|\,\equiv\,0\!\!\!\pmod{2}}\,\frac{Q_{\underline{k}}\!\left(|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}};|p|^{\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2}\right)}{|p|^{k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}+k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}+k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}+k_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}}\bigg).

Note that the local factor of the product in the right-hand side is

\sum_{|\underline{k}|\,\equiv\,0\!\!\!\!\pmod{2}}Q_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}(x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}};u)\underline{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\underline{k}$}}

where, for a monic irreducible
p,
we set

x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}\!=|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}

(
i=1,\ldots,5
), and
u=|p|^{\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2}.

By (
35
), this local
factor evaluated at

x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}=u

is just
R(u\underline{x};u),
and thus

\lim_{s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\to\frac{1}{2}}\!\big(1-q^{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{1}{2}$}-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\big)\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};1,a_{\scriptscriptstyle 1})=\prod_{p}R\big(|p|^{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}$}-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\!,|p|^{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}$}-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\!,|p|^{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}$}-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}}\!,|p|^{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}$}-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}};|p|^{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle-\frac{1}{2}$}}\big).

By using the formula of
R(\underline{x};u)
in Theorem
6.1
,
the product equals

R(q^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\!,q^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}}\!,q^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 3$}}}\!,q^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}};\sqrt{q}),

that is,
\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})
and

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})

have the same residue at
x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}=1/\penalty\sqrt{q}.
This shows that

\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})=\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q}).

To prove the second equality, set
u=\sqrt{q}
. It is clear that

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(u\mathbf{x};u)
satisfies
the conditions (a) and (b) inthe
previous section. The upper bound in condition (c) follows
easily from (
12
) and the fact that the
coeffi-cients of the power series expansion of

J(x/\penalty u,u,\varepsilon)
are polynomials in

u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}
.

To show that the coefficients of

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(u\mathbf{x};u)

satisfy the dominance condition, we have to show that
the coef-ficients
a(\mathrm{k};u)

in (
40
) are divisible by

u^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}
if
|\mathrm{k}|>1
.
This follows from Corollary
5.4
.

The first and third conditions in (A1) can be verified using
Lemma
5.2
; the generating function of

b(\underline{k},1;q)
can be computed from the term

1|\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}(\mathbf{x};u)
in
Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};u)
.
Conditions (A2) and (A3) can be easily verified directly.
This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 8.1
.

​The fact that

\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})

coincides with

Z(q^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2$}}\mathbf{x};q)

shows that our choice of the
p
-part
\widetilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}
for the Weyl group multiple Dirichlet series associated with the
4
-th moment of quadratic
L
-functions is canonical; this comparison was the sole reason for introducing the axiomatic multiple Dirichlet series at the end of the previous section.
The uniqueness of
Z(\mathbf{x};q)
implies, for example, that the multiple Dirichlet series with all the
p
-parts equal to

Z_{\scriptscriptstyle W}^{\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{CG}}\big(|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}},\ldots,|p|^{-s_{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}};|p|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2$}}\big)

cannot satisfy the full set of conditions that
\mathscr{Z}\!(\mathbf{x})
satisfies. In fact, the reader can check directly using
(
45
) in Remark
7.2
that condition (A3)
(i.e., the local-to-global principle)
is
not
satisfied for this choice of the
p
-parts.

9
Applications

In this final section of the paper, we begin with two straightforward
consequences of Theorem
8.1
. First, by taking
coefficients of the function

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})

with respect to the variable

x_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}
, we obtain an exact
for-mula for the 4-th moments of quadratic Dirichlet

L
-functions over rational function fields, ​weighted bythe
polynomials
P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}
defined
in Section
7
and Lemma
5.2
. ​Then,
from the analytic properties of

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})
,we also deduce an asymptotic formula for these moments,
​completely analogous to that conjectured in

[
28
]
for arbitrary moments. For the remaining
of the section, we study in some detail the secondary terms in
the asymptotic formula. In particular, our analysis will show
that all these terms are non-zero.

Theorem 9.1
.

— For
D\geqslant 1,
we have the exact formula:

\sum_{\deg d\,=D}L\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}\right)^{4}\!P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})=\mathrm{Coeff}_{\xi^{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}}\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\underline{1},\xi;\sqrt{q})

where

P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})=P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(0,\ldots,0;\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}),

and
(\underline{1},\xi):=(1,1,1,1,\xi).

Proof.

If we put

\xi=q^{-s_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}
,
then by (
42
) we can write

\mathscr{W}\!(\mathbf{s};1,1\!)\,=\,\sum_{D\,\geqslant\,0}\left\{\sum_{\deg d\,=D}\;\prod_{i=1}^{4}L\!\left(s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}+\tfrac{1}{2},\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}\right)P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\mathbf{s}\scalebox{1.}{${}^{\prime}$};\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})\right\}\xi^{D}.

Setting
s_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=0,
the asserted formula follows
at once from the equality

\mathscr{Z}\!(\underline{1},\xi)=\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\underline{1},\xi;\sqrt{q}).

∎

However, one cannot expect to have an analogue of this result for
general function fields, or number fields.For this reason, we shall also give the asymptotic formula for the
fourth moment sums that is, indeed, ex-pected to generalize to any
global field. This asymptotic formula has also the advantage of
separating thecontributions corresponding to the
singularities of the function
\mathscr{Z}\!(\underline{1},\xi).

For
n\geqslant 1,
let, as in
[
28
, Section 6]
,

\Phi_{n}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{5}n_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in\Phi_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}:n_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}=n\right\}

and, for
D\geqslant 1,
define
Q_{n}(D,q)
by

Q_{n}(D,q)=\lim_{\underline{x}\,\to\,\underline{1}}\bigg(\sum_{\alpha\,\in\,\Phi_{n}}\,\sum_{\zeta^{2n}=1}R_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};\sqrt{q})\zeta^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}\mathbf{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}/\penalty n}\bigg)

where
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha-n\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5},
and

R_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};\sqrt{q})

is given by (
34
). We have the following:

Theorem 9.2
.

— For
D,N\geqslant 1
and

(N+1)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}<\Theta<N^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}\!,

we have the asymptotic formula

\sum_{\deg d\,=D}L\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}\right)^{4}\!P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})\,=\sum_{n\,\leqslant\,N}Q_{n}(D,q)q^{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{D}{2n}$}}\,+\,O_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Theta$},\,q}\left(q^{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\frac{D\Theta}{2}$}}\right).

Proof.

The asymptotic formula follows from Theorem

8.1
and a straightforward application of the residue
theorem to the integral

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\oint_{\partial\mathscr{A}_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Theta$}}}\!\frac{\mathscr{Z}\!(\underline{1},\xi)}{\xi^{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle+1$}}}\,d\xi

where

\mathscr{A}_{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle\Theta$}}=\{\xi\in\mathbb{C}:q^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-2$}}\leqslant|\xi|\leqslant q^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-\Theta/\penalty 2$}}\}
— see also
[
28
, Theorem 6.1]
.
∎

Remark 9.1
.

One might be puzzled by the discrepancy of a factor of

q^{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty 2$}}

in this asymptotic formula. ​This is simply explained by our
normalization

\mathbf{x}\to q^{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2$}}\mathbf{x}

of the function

\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}(\mathbf{x};\sqrt{q})

which is causing the cor-rection polynomials

P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})

to be off by a factor of
|d|^{\scalebox{1.}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2$}}.

For instance, when

d=d_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}

is square-free, then

P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})=|d|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2$}}

instead of
1.

Notice also that Corollary
5.5
and definition (
43
)
of the Dirichlet polynomials

P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(s_{\scriptscriptstyle 1},\ldots,s_{\scriptscriptstyle 4};\chi_{a_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})

imply that

P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})

is non-negative for all
d
. In particular, we have the inequality

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d-\mathrm{monic\;\&\;sq.\,free}\\
\deg d\,=D\end{subarray}}L\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\chi_{d}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}|d|^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2$}}\,\leqslant\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d-\mathrm{monic}\\
\deg d\,=D\end{subarray}}L\!\left(\tfrac{1}{2},\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}\!P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}}).

Moreover,
[
28
, Conjecture 1.2]
predicts an asymptotic formula
for the (traditional) moment sum in the left-hand side
of the above inequality, similar to that in Theorem
9.2
.
(This similarity between the two asymp-totics should still persist
when considering the analogues of these moment sums over any global field.)
For these reasons, the presence of the correction factors

P_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle d$}}(\chi_{d_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle 0$}}})

in our fourth moment sum is harmless for all practical purposes.
That is, if an analogue of the asymptotic formula in Theorem

9.2
is proved in thenumber field setting, it would have
the same applications as the corresponding asymptotic formula for
thetraditional fourth moment sum.

9.1
An explicit formula for
Q_{n}(D,q)

We now give explicit formulas for the terms
Q_{n}(D,q)

in Theorem
9.2
, following closely
[
28
]
.
In
loc. cit.
,conjectural formulas were given for the first two
terms
Q_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
Q_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
in the asymptotics of
the
r
-th moment ofquadratic Dirichlet
L
-functions,
summed over square-free monic polynomials. It is
interesting to note thatwe obtain the same formulas, except
for the so-called “arithmetic factor” which is simpler when summing
over all monic polynomials. Here, we are able to treat all the terms

Q_{n}(D,q)
since the sets
\Phi_{n}

can be ex-plicitly described
for
D_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}^{\scriptscriptstyle(1)}
.

First, we rewrite the double sum in the formula of
Q_{n}(D,u^{2})

by grouping together the terms with
\pm\zeta
. De-note by

f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e}$}}=f^{+}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
(resp. ​
f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o}$}}=f^{-}_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
) the even
(resp. ​odd) part of the function
f(\mathbf{x})
with respect to the sign
func-tion
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
, and let
\mu_{\scriptscriptstyle k}
be the set
of
k
-th roots of unity in
\mathbb{C}
. We have

Q_{n}(D,u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\zeta\in\mu_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2n$}}/\penalty\{\pm 1\}}\zeta^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}\!I_{n,\,\zeta}(D,u)

with

I_{n,\,\zeta}(D,u)=\lim_{\underline{x}\,\to\,\underline{1}}\sum_{\alpha\,\in\,\Phi_{n}}\mathbf{x}^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}/\penalty n}\!\!\left.\left\{R(w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\mathbf{x};u)f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}^{a_{\scalebox{.70}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}}\!(\mathbf{x};u)\right\}\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.75}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}

(47)

where
f_{w}=(1+ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})\|w
was defined in Lemma
6.2
, and

a_{\scriptscriptstyle D}\in\{\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}\}
denotes the parity of
D
.
Although the function
R
does not depend on
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
, we write

R(\mathbf{x};u)
for
R(\underline{x};u)
, hence
R(w\underline{x};u)=R(w\mathbf{x};u)
.
Notice that
R(w\mathbf{x};u)
is even with respect to both

\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and
\varepsilon_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
for all
w\in W
,
and that

I_{n,-\zeta}(D,u)=(-1)^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}I_{n,\,\zeta}(D,u)
,
so the sum over
\zeta
above is indeed well-defined.

Next, we give integral formulas for the sum in (
47
),
from which it will be clear that the limit exists,
and it isa polynomial in
D
of degree 10 if
n
is odd,
and of degree 7 if
n
is even.

9.1.1
The case
n
odd

Let
n=2k+1
with
k\geqslant 0
.
For
I\subset S:=\{1,2,3,4\},
possibly empty, put

w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}=\prod_{\scriptscriptstyle i\in I}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i},
and

\alpha_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}=\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i},

with the understanding that the empty product is the identity,
and the empty sum is 0. Also, let

t=\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cdots\,\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
,
for which
t\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}-\delta
. Then

\Phi_{n}=\{\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}+\alpha_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}+k\delta:I\subset S\}\;\;\;\,\text{and}\;\;\;\,t^{k}w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}(\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}+\alpha_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}+k\delta)=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}.

Notice that
t^{2}
is a translation, with

t^{2}\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i}=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i}+\delta
(
i=1,\ldots,4
),
and
t^{2}\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}-2\delta
.

Lemma 9.3
.

—
Let
n=2k+1
with
k\geqslant 0,
and let
\alpha=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}+k\delta

and
w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}=t^{k}
. ​Then we
have a decomposition

R(w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\mathbf{x};u)|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.75}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}=R_{n,\,\zeta}(\underline{x};u)\,\cdot\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant 4}\frac{1}{1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}x_{\scriptscriptstyle j}}

with

R_{n,\,\zeta}(\underline{1};u)=R_{n}(u^{-2/\penalty n}/\penalty\zeta^{2})

for an explicit function
R_{n}(\varrho)
. Moreover, the function

R_{n}(\varrho)
is given by anabsolutely convergent power
series for
|\varrho|<1;
when
n=1,
we have

R_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\varrho)=(\varrho;\varrho)_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle-11}.

Due to Remark
9.2
below, we omit the formula
for
R_{n}(\varrho)
when
n>1.

Proof.

The assertion follows at once from the explicit formula of

R(\underline{x};u)
in Theorem
6.1
.
∎

Recall that
f_{w}=(1+ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})\|w
. For
b\in\{\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}\}
, let

f_{n,\,\zeta}^{b}(\underline{x};u)=f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}^{b}\!(\mathbf{x};u)|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.75}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}

for
\alpha
and
w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}

asin the previous lemma. Since
\alpha
and
w_{\alpha}

are fixed, we omit them from the notation

R_{n,\,\zeta}
and
f_{n,\,\zeta}^{b}
.

Proposition 9.4
.

— The limit (
47
) is given by the
integral

I_{n,\,\zeta}(D,u)=\frac{1}{4!}\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{{}^{\!4}}}\!\oint\cdots\oint h_{n,\,\zeta,\,D}(\underline{z};u)\frac{\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 4}(z_{\scriptscriptstyle j}-z_{\scriptscriptstyle i})^{{}^{\!2}}\!(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}z_{\scriptscriptstyle j})}{\prod_{i=1}^{4}(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle i})^{{}^{\!8}}}\frac{dz_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}}{z_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}}\cdots\frac{dz_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}}{z_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}}

where

h_{n,\,\zeta,\,D}(\underline{z};u)=\frac{R_{n,\,\zeta}(\underline{z};u)}{\prod_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty(2n)}}\cdot\begin{cases}f_{n,\,\zeta}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e}$}}(\underline{z};u)\prod_{i=1}^{4}\dfrac{1-u}{1-u/\penalty z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}&\text{if $D$ even}\\
f_{n,\,\zeta}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o}$}}(\underline{z};u)\prod_{i=1}^{4}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2}&\text{if $D$ odd}.\end{cases}

Here each path of integration encloses the point

z_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=1
, but not the points
z_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=0,u
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi_{n}
and

w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\in W

be as in the previous lemma.
​By taking
\beta=\alpha+\alpha_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}\in\Phi_{n}
in (
47
), with

w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\beta$}}=w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}
, we have

f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}w_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}}(\mathbf{x})=f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}\|w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i\,\in\,I}\frac{1-u/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}{1-ux_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e}$}}\!(w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}\mathbf{x})+\frac{1}{\mathbf{x}^{\alpha_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}}}f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o}$}}\!(w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}\mathbf{x}).

Assuming
D
is even (the case
D
odd being similar), we can then
write the sum in (
47
) as

\prod_{i=1}^{4}(1-u/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle i})x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty 2}\sum_{I\subset S}\left.h(w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}\mathbf{x};u)\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha_{\scalebox{0.55}{$\scriptscriptstyle I$}}+k\alpha_{\scalebox{0.55}{$\scriptscriptstyle S$}}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}

where

h(\mathbf{x};u)=\frac{R(w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\mathbf{x};u)f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e}$}}\!(\mathbf{x};u)}{\prod_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}(1-u/\penalty x_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}})x_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}^{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty(2n)}}
. Now we use
the following:

Fact 9.5
.

— For any function
g(\mathbf{x})
and a fixed index
1\leqslant j\leqslant 4
, the transformation

x_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\mapsto 1/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
takes

g(\mathbf{x})|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,c/\penalty x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}}^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}}\xrightarrow{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}}\mapsto 1/\penalty x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}}}g(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\mathbf{x})|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,c/\penalty x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}}^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle b$}}}

where
a,b\in\mathbb{Q}
with
a+b=1
, and
c
is any function not
depending on
x_{\scriptscriptstyle j}
and
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
.

Taking for
g(\mathbf{x})
the function
h(\mathbf{x};u)
and applying

[
28
, Lemma 7.1]
(the case
m=0
of Lemma
9.9
below)
yields an integral representation for the sum above.
Now one can take
\underline{x}\to\underline{1}
inside the integral,
giving theabove expression for
I_{n,\,\zeta}(D,u)
.
∎

From the integral representation, a standard argument (e.g.,

[
28
, Prop. 7.7]
) leads to the following:

Corollary 9.6
.

— When
n
is odd,
I_{n,\,\zeta}(D,u)
is a
polynomial in
D
of degree 10, with leading term given by

D^{10}R_{n,\,\zeta}(\underline{1};u)f_{n,\,\zeta}^{a_{\scalebox{.70}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}}(\underline{1};u)\frac{1}{4!(2n)^{{}^{\!10}}}\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{{}^{\!4}}}\!\oint_{|t_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}|=1}\cdots\oint_{|t_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}|=1}\prod_{i=1}^{4}e^{-t_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}}}\cdot\frac{\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 4}(t_{\scriptscriptstyle i}-t_{\scriptscriptstyle j})^{{}^{\!2}}\!(t_{\scriptscriptstyle i}+t_{\scriptscriptstyle j})}{\prod_{i=1}^{4}t_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle 8}}dt_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cdots\,dt_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}.

The integral (with the factor
(2\pi i)^{-4}
included) equals

8/\penalty 1575
. When
n=1
, following
[
36
, Prop. 2.1]
,
we can express the leading term of
Q_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(D,q)
as

D^{10}R_{1}(q^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})\frac{1}{2^{r}}\prod_{j=0}^{r-1}\frac{(2j)!}{(r+j)!}\;\,\quad\;\,(r=4).

It agrees with the analogous main term in the conjectural
asymptotic formula of the fourth moment over the rationals
(see
[
24
]
,
[
21
]
, or
[
36
, Theorem 1.1]
),
apart from the “arithmetic factor”​, which in our case
is
R_{1}(\varrho)=\left(\varrho;\varrho\right)_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle-11}
.

9.1.2
The case
n
even

Let
n=2k
with
k\geqslant 1
, and let

t=\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\cdots\,\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}

be as before, with
t\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}-\delta
. Then

\Phi_{n}=\{\pm\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i}+k\delta:i\in S\}\;\;\;\,\text{and}\;\;\;\,t^{k-1}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}t(-\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i}+k\delta)=t^{k-1}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}t\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle i}(\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle i}+k\delta)=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}.

Lemma 9.7
.

— Let
n=2k
with
k\geqslant 1,
and let

\alpha=-\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}+k\delta\in\Phi_{n}
and

w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}=t^{k-1}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}t
. Then we have a decom-position

R(w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\mathbf{x};u)|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}=R_{n,\,\zeta}(\underline{x};u)\,\cdot\,\frac{1}{1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\prod_{j=1}^{3}\frac{1}{(1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}x_{\scriptscriptstyle j})(1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle j})}

with

R_{n,\,\zeta}(\underline{1};u)=R_{n}(u^{-2/\penalty n}/\penalty\zeta^{2})

for an explicit function
R_{n}(\varrho)
. In addition, the
function
R_{n}(\varrho)
is given by an absolutely convergent power
series for
|\varrho|<1
; when
n=2,
we have

R_{2}(\varrho)=\left(\varrho;\varrho\right)_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle-8}\left(\varrho;\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\right)_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle-6}(1-1/\penalty\varrho)^{\scriptscriptstyle-7}.

Proof.

The assertion follows at once from the explicit formula
of
R(\underline{x};u)
in Theorem
6.1
.
∎

For

b\in\{\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}\}
,
let

f_{n,\,\zeta}^{b}(\underline{x};u)=f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}^{b}\!(\mathbf{x};u)|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.75}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}

for
\alpha
and
w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}
as in the
previous lemma.

Proposition 9.8
.

— The limit

I_{n,\,\zeta}(D,u)
is given by the integral

\frac{1}{2^{{}^{3}}3!}\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{{}^{\!4}}}\!\oint\cdots\oint h_{n,\,\zeta,\,D}(\underline{z};u)\frac{\prod_{1\leqslant i<j\leqslant 4}(z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}-z_{\scriptscriptstyle j})^{\mathrm{e}_{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle ij$}}}(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}z_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\prod_{1\leqslant k\leqslant l\leqslant 3}(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle k}z_{\scriptscriptstyle l})\prod_{i=1}^{3}\!z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}{\prod_{i=1}^{4}(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle i})^{{}^{\!8}}}\frac{dz_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}}{z_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}}\cdots\frac{dz_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}}{z_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}}

where

\mathrm{e}_{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle ij$}}=1

or
2
according as
j=4
or not, and

h_{n,\,\zeta,\,D}(\underline{z};u)=\frac{R_{n,\,\zeta}(\underline{z};u)}{z_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}^{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty n}}\cdot\begin{cases}f_{n,\,\zeta}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e}$}}(\underline{z};u)\prod_{i=1}^{4}\dfrac{1-u}{1-u/\penalty z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}}&\text{if $D$ even}\\
f_{n,\,\zeta}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o}$}}(\underline{z};u)\prod_{i=1}^{4}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2}&\text{if $D$ odd}.\end{cases}

The paths of integration are as in Proposition
9.4
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\Phi_{n}
and

w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\in W
be as in the
previous lemma, and set

\alpha^{+}\!=\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\alpha\in\Phi_{n}
,
and

w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha^{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}$}}\!=w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
.
As before, assume that
D
is even, as the case
D
odd
is entirely similar. We have

f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha^{\scalebox{.6}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}$}}}\!=f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}\|\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
,
and the two terms of the sum in (
47
)
corresponding to
\alpha
and
\alpha^{+}
can be written as

\prod_{i=1}^{4}(1-u/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle i})x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty 2}\cdot\left(\left.h(\mathbf{x};u)\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}+\left.h(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\mathbf{x};u)\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha^{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}\right)

where

h(\mathbf{x};u)=\frac{R(w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\mathbf{x};u)f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e}$}}\!(\mathbf{x};u)}{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}^{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty n}\prod_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}(1-u/\penalty x_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}})}

and
\alpha^{+^{\prime}}
​denotes the sum of the first four
components of
\alpha^{+}
.
By Fact
9.5
, the two terms inside the parenthesis
are interchanged by the transformation

x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\mapsto 1/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}
. Moreover,
the function
h(\mathbf{x};u)
is clearly symmetric
in the first three variables, and
we claim that the first term in the parenthesis is also invariant
under the transformations

x_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\mapsto 1/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle j},

for
j=1,2,3
. Indeed, fixing one such
j
, by applying again
Fact
9.5
, we have that

\left.h(\mathbf{x};u)\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}\xrightarrow{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}}\mapsto 1/\penalty x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}}}\left.h(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\mathbf{x};u)\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}.

One checks that

h(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\mathbf{x};u)=\frac{R(w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\sigma_{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}}\mathbf{x};u)f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\sigma_{\!\scalebox{.55}{$\scriptscriptstyle j$}}}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e}$}}\!(\mathbf{x};u)}{x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}^{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty n}\prod_{\scriptscriptstyle i=1}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}(1-u/\penalty x_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle i$}})}
.
Since

w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\alpha=w_{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}\alpha=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
,
our claim follows now by Lemma
6.2
.

Using these symmetries and the lemma below with
m=3
,
we can express the sum in (
47
) as an integral. Our
assertion follows now by taking
\underline{x}\to 1
inside the integral.
∎

Lemma 9.9
.

— Let

a_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,a_{r}

be distinct non-zero complex numbers such that
a_{\scriptscriptstyle i}a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\neq 1
for all

1\leqslant i,j\leqslant r.
Suppose
h
is a function of
r
complex variables, holomorphic on a domain containing

\Big(a_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{\delta_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}}\!,\ldots,a_{r}^{\delta_{r}}\Big)

for all

(\delta_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,\delta_{r})\in\{\pm 1\}^{r}.

For
0\leqslant m<r,
define
K_{m}(\mathrm{z})
by

K_{m}(\mathrm{z})=\frac{h(\mathrm{z})}{\prod_{k=1}^{m}\prod_{l=m+1}^{r}(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle k}z_{\scriptscriptstyle l})(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle k}^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}z_{\scriptscriptstyle l})\prod_{m+1\,\leqslant\,k\,\leqslant\,l\,\leqslant\,r}(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle k}z_{\scriptscriptstyle l})},\quad\mathrm{z}:=(z_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}},\ldots,z_{r}).

Then we have

\begin{split}&\sum_{\sigma\,\in\,\mathbb{S}_{r}}\;\sum_{\delta_{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma(i)$}}\,=\,\pm 1}K_{m}\Big(a_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma(1)$}}^{\delta_{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma(1)$}}}\!,\ldots,a_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma(r)$}}^{\delta_{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\sigma(r)$}}}\!\Big)\\
&=\frac{(-1)^{r(r+1)/\penalty 2}}{\left(2\pi\sqrt{-1}\right)^{r}}\!\oint\!\cdots\!\oint h(\mathrm{z})\cdot\frac{\prod_{1\,\leqslant\,i\,<\,j\,\leqslant\,r}\,(z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}-z_{\scriptscriptstyle j})^{\mathrm{e}_{\scalebox{.65}{$\scriptscriptstyle ij$}}}(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}z_{\scriptscriptstyle j})\,\cdot\,\prod_{1\,\leqslant\,k\,\leqslant\,l\,\leqslant\,m}(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle k}z_{\scriptscriptstyle l})\prod_{i=1}^{m}z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}^{\scriptscriptstyle r-m}}{\prod_{i,\,j=1}^{r}\big(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}\big)\big(1-z_{\scriptscriptstyle i}a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\big)}\frac{d\mathrm{z}}{\mathrm{z}^{\scriptscriptstyle r}}\end{split}

where
\mathrm{e}_{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle ij$}}=1
or
2
according as
i\leqslant m
and
j\geqslant m+1
or not,

\mathrm{z}^{r}:=z_{\scalebox{.98}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}^{r}\cdots\,z_{r}^{r},
and where
each path of integration encloses the
a_{\scriptscriptstyle j}^{\scriptscriptstyle\pm 1},
but not

z_{\scriptscriptstyle j}=0.

Proof.

The same idea as in the proof of
[
28
, Lemma 7.5]
.
∎

The argument in
[
28
, Prop. 7.7]
gives the following:

Corollary 9.10
.

— When
n
is even,
I_{n,\,\zeta}(D,u)
is a polynomial
of degree 7 in
D
with leading term

\frac{D^{\scriptscriptstyle 7}}{7!n^{\scriptscriptstyle 7}}R_{n}(\zeta^{-2}u^{-2/\penalty n})f_{n,\,\zeta}^{a_{\scalebox{.70}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}}(\underline{1};u).

Remark 9.2
.

We omitted providing formulas for the functions

R_{n}(\varrho)
in Lemmas
9.3
and
9.7
,
when
n\geqslant 3.
Instead, we will show in the next subsection that the leading term of

Q_{n}(D,u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
can be solely expressed interms of
R_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\varrho)
or
R_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\varrho)
(according as
n
is odd or
even), and of the matrix
B(\mathbf{x};u)
in Theorem
4.3
.

9.1.3
The leading term of
Q_{n}(D,q)

The leading term of
Q_{n}(D,q)
, as a polynomial in
D
, is

\frac{D^{\scriptscriptstyle 10}}{2^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}n^{\scriptscriptstyle 10}}\prod_{j=0}^{3}\frac{(2j)!}{(4+j)!}S_{n}(D,\sqrt{q})\;\;\;\text{or}\;\;\;\frac{D^{\scriptscriptstyle 7}}{7!n^{\scriptscriptstyle 7}}S_{n}(D,\sqrt{q})

according as
n
is odd or even. Here we set

S_{n}(D,u):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\zeta\in\mu_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2n$}}/\penalty\{\pm 1\}}\zeta^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}R_{n}(\zeta^{-2}u^{-2/\penalty n})f_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}^{a_{\scalebox{.70}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}}\!(\underline{1},\zeta^{-1}u^{-1/\penalty n};u)

where
\alpha\in\Phi_{n}
,
w_{\alpha}\in W
, and the
functions
R_{n}(\varrho)
are those occurring
in Lemmas
9.3
and
9.7
. Recall that

a_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}\in\{\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}\}
stands for the parity of
D
,
and that
f^{\mathcal{e}}=f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}+f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{e}$}}
,
f^{\mathcal{o}}=f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}

for
f\in\mathbb{C}(\mathbf{x},u)_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
.

In what follows, we show that the functions

S_{n}(D,\sqrt{q})
are non-zero for all
n\geqslant 1

and all
D
, so that all
Q_{n}(D,q)
are present in the
asymptotic formula in Theorem
9.2
.
In fact, we prove the stronger result that

S_{n}(D,\sqrt{q})
is given by a power series in
q^{-1/\penalty 2n}
,
whose coefficients are either all positive or all negative,
depending on the residue of
n
modulo
4
.

We start by giving an expression for
S_{n}(D,u)

involving only the term for
\zeta=1
in the summation.
​To do so, we need a property of the function
f_{w}=(1+ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5})\|w
.

Lemma 9.11
.

— For all
w\in W
, the functions

f_{w}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}
,
f_{w}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}
and
f_{w}^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{e}$}}
are even, odd and
odd, respectively, as func-tions of
u
.

Proof.

We have

\overline{f_{w}}(\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{\Lambda}_{w}(\mathbf{x};u)\cdot\,\!^{t}(1,u\mathbf{x}^{w^{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}\alpha_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}}\!,0).

(48)

As functions of
u
, the entries of
\tilde{\Lambda}_{w}(\mathbf{x};u)
have the same parities
as the corresponding entries of the matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&u&u\\
u&1&1\\
u&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\!.

This follows easily by induction on the length
\ell(w)
of
w
; the
case
\ell(w)=1
is clear from the formulas of
\Lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}
and

\Lambda_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
in Section
4.1
, and the product of such matrices
is again of the same type, so the co-cycle relation gives the induction
step. Our assertions now follow from (
48
).
∎

For
c\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
\mathcal{U}_{n,\,c}
be the operator acting on Laurent
series
f(\varrho)=\sum_{k\,\in\,\mathbb{Z}}n_{\scriptscriptstyle k}\varrho^{k}
with
n_{\scriptscriptstyle k}\in\mathbb{C}
,
defined by

\mathcal{U}_{n,\,c}(f)\;:=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\,\equiv\,c\!\!\!\!\pmod{n}\end{subarray}}n_{\scriptscriptstyle k}\varrho^{k}=\;\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\zeta\in\mu_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle n$}}}\zeta^{c}f(\zeta^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}\varrho).

We will apply this operator to Laurent series
f
which
converge absolutely for
0<|\varrho|<1
, when it is well-defined and the equality above clearly holds.
Note that
\mathcal{U}_{n,\,c}
depends only on
c
modulo
n
.

Proposition 9.12
.

—
With
\alpha
,
w_{\alpha}
and
R_{n}(\varrho)
from Lemma
9.3

(resp. Lemma
9.7
) for
n
odd (resp.
n
even),
wehave:

S_{n}(D,u)=\mathcal{U}_{n,\,{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty 2}}\!\left[R_{n}(u^{-2/\penalty n})\cdot f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}_{w_{\alpha}}(\underline{1},u^{-1/\penalty n};u)\right]+u\mathcal{U}_{n,\,{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty 2}}\!\left[R_{n}(u^{-2/\penalty n})\cdot u^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{e}$}}_{w_{\alpha}}(\underline{1},u^{-1/\penalty n};u)\right]

if
D
is even, and

S_{n}(D,u)=u^{1-1/\penalty n}\,\mathcal{U}_{n,\,{\scriptscriptstyle\lfloor D/\penalty 2\rfloor}}\!\left[R_{n}(u^{-2/\penalty n})\cdot u^{-1+1/\penalty n}f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}_{w_{\alpha}}(\underline{1},u^{-1/\penalty n};u)\right]

if
D
is odd. Here we view the functions between brackets
as Laurent series in
\varrho=u^{-2/\penalty n}
, by the previous lemma.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.11
, we have that

f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{e}$}}_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};u)=g_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2},u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}),\;\;\;\,f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{e},\mathcal{o}$}}_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};u)=ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}g_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2},u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})\;\;\;\,\text{and}\;\;\;\,f^{\scalebox{1.2}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{o},\mathcal{e}$}}_{w_{\scalebox{.9}{$\scriptscriptstyle\alpha$}}}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};u)=ug_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2},u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})

with
g_{\scriptscriptstyle i}\in\mathbb{Q}(x,u)
. Thus, assuming
D
to be even
(the case
D
odd being similar), we have the following
ex-pression for
S_{n}(D,u)
:

\displaystyle S_{n}(D,u)=S_{n}(D,\varrho^{-n/\penalty 2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{\zeta\in\mu_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2n$}}/\penalty\{\pm 1\}}\zeta^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle D$}}R_{n}(\varrho/\penalty\zeta^{2})\big(g_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\varrho/\penalty\zeta^{2},\varrho^{-n})+ug_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(\varrho/\penalty\zeta^{2},\varrho^{-n})\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{U}_{n,\,{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty 2}}[R_{n}(\varrho)g_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\varrho,\varrho^{-n})]+u\mathcal{U}_{n,\,{\scriptscriptstyle D/\penalty 2}}[R_{n}(\varrho)g_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(\varrho,\varrho^{-n})].

Note that the functions between brackets converge absolutely as
Laurent series in
\varrho
for
0<|\varrho|<1
,
by Lemmas
9.3
and
9.7
, so the last
equality holds. Switching back to the variable
u

completes the proof.
∎

This proposition yields explicit formulas for the leading term
of
Q_{n}(D,q)
, which, for small
n
, can be usedto check that this term is given by a non-zero power series
with coefficients of the same sign. To prove thisproperty for all
n
, in Corollary
9.15
below, we shall
give different expressions for the functions between brackets
in the proposition, using the extra functional equation
introduced in Section
4.2
.

Recall the extension of the cocycle
\tilde{\Lambda}
to

W\oplus\mathbb{Z}
from the beginning of Section
6.3
.
In particular, we have

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-1}}(\mathbf{x};u)=\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u)=B(\mathbf{x};u)/\penalty(1-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}x^{\delta})^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}

and the vector function

\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}=\overline{\tilde{Z}_{\scriptscriptstyle W}}

satisfies the functional equation (
25
), with the cocycle

\tilde{\Lambda}
in place of
\Lambda
.

Consider the vector version of the residue in Lemma
6.2
:

\mathbf{C}_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u):=\hskip-44.0pt\lim_{\hskip 44.0ptx_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\to\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.75}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}\!\left(1-\zeta u^{1/\penalty n}\mathbf{x}^{\alpha/\penalty n}\right)\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}(\mathbf{x};u)

for any

\alpha=n\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}+\alpha\scalebox{1.1}{${}^{\prime}$}\in\Phi_{n}

and

\zeta\in\mu_{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2n$}}
.
The residue vector

\mathbf{C}_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u)

appears in the proof of Lemma
6.2
, where it is shown that

\mathbf{C}_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u)=\frac{1}{2n}\left.\!\left\{R(w_{\alpha}\underline{x};u)\overline{f_{w_{\alpha}}}(\mathbf{x};u)\right\}\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}

(49)

with
w_{\alpha}\in W
such that
w_{\alpha}\alpha=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
.

Assuming now that
\alpha
is chosen as in
Lemmas
9.3
and
9.7
, we give a formula for

\mathbf{C}_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u)

in terms of the above extended cocycle.

Lemma 9.13
.

— a). If
n=2k+1
and

\alpha=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}+k\delta\in\Phi_{n}
, then

\mathbf{C}_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u)=\frac{1}{2n}\left.\!\left\{R(\underline{x};u\mathbf{x}^{k\delta})\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-k}}(\mathbf{x};u)\cdot v_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}\right\}\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}

where
v_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}=\,\!^{t}(1,1,0)
.

b). If
n=2k+2
and
\alpha=-\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}+(k+1)\delta\in\Phi_{n}
, then

\mathbf{C}_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u)=\frac{1}{2n}\left.\!\left\{R(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}t\underline{x};u\mathbf{x}^{k\delta})\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-k}}(\mathbf{x};u)\cdot\overline{f_{\sigma_{\scalebox{.67}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}t}}(\mathbf{x};u\mathbf{x}^{k\delta})\right\}\right|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}

where
t=\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
.

Proof.

We use the functional equation (
25
), written for

\mathbf{\tilde{Z}}
and the cocycle
\tilde{\Lambda}
, to compute the
residue vector

\mathbf{C}_{\alpha,\,\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle\zeta$}}(\underline{x};u)
.
In part a), we take the element
\tau^{-k}
in (
25
),
while in part b) we take the element
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}t\tau^{-k}
.
Note that by Theorem
4.3
and the cocycle relation,
the matrix
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-k}}(\mathbf{x};u)
has poles only if

u\mathbf{x}^{\beta}=\pm 1
for some

\beta\in\Phi_{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathrm{re}$}}^{\scalebox{.95}{$\scriptscriptstyle+$}}

with
\beta<k\delta
. Since
\alpha>k\delta
, it can be evaluated at

x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}\,=\,\zeta^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}
,
that is, when
u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha}=\pm 1
.
∎

The decompositions in Lemmas
9.3
and
9.7

have the following analogues:

Lemma 9.14
.

— a). For
n=2k+1\geqslant 1

and
\alpha=\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}+k\delta\in\Phi_{n}
, we have

R(\underline{x};u\mathbf{x}^{k\delta})|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}=R_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,\,1}\!\left(\underline{x};u\mathbf{x}^{k\delta}|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}\right)\,\cdot\,\prod_{1\leqslant i\leqslant j\leqslant 4}\frac{1}{1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle i}x_{\scriptscriptstyle j}}

where the first factor in the right-hand side becomes

R_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(u^{-2/\penalty n}/\penalty\zeta^{2})
when evaluated
at
\underline{x}=\underline{1}
. Here
R_{\scriptscriptstyle 1,\,1}(\underline{x};u)
and

R_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\varrho)
are the same functions as in Lemma
9.3
.

b). For
n=2k+2\geqslant 2
and
\alpha=-\alpha_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}+(k+1)\delta\in\Phi_{n}
,
we have

R(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}t\underline{x};u\mathbf{x}^{k\delta})|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}=R_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,\,1}\!\left(\underline{x};u\mathbf{x}^{k\delta}|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}\right)\,\cdot\,\frac{1}{1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}}\prod_{j=1}^{3}\frac{1}{(1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}x_{\scriptscriptstyle j})(1-x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle j})}

where the first factor in the right-hand side becomes

R_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(u^{-2/\penalty n}/\penalty\zeta^{2})
when evaluated
at
\underline{x}=\underline{1}
. Here
R_{\scriptscriptstyle 2,\,1}(\underline{x};u)
and
R_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\varrho)

are the same functions as in Lemma
9.7
.

Proof.

The assertions follow at once from the cases

n=1
,
\zeta=1
and
n=2
,
\zeta=1
of the two
aforementioned lemmas, after making the substitution

u\mapsto u\mathbf{x}^{k\delta}|_{x_{\scalebox{.75}{$\scriptscriptstyle 5$}}\,=\,\zeta^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}/\penalty n}}=\begin{cases}\zeta^{{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}}-n}(u\mathbf{x}^{\alpha\scalebox{.9}{${}^{\prime}$}})^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1$}/\penalty n}&\text{if $n$ odd}\\
\zeta^{{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}-n}x_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}^{{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1-2$}}/\penalty n}u^{{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 2$}}/\penalty n}&\text{if $n$ even}.\end{cases}

Note that
R(\underline{x};u)
is even in
u
, and so the factors

\zeta^{n}=\pm 1
can be ignored in the above substitution.
∎

We now give simpler formulas for the functions inside
brackets in Proposition
9.12
, in which the dependence
upon
n
appears only in the cocycle matrix

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-\lfloor(n-1)/\penalty 2\rfloor}}
.
We express the arguments in terms of

\varrho=

u^{{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-2$}}/\penalty n}
,
choosing the square root

\varrho^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2$}}:=u^{{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1$}}/\penalty n}
.

Corollary 9.15
.

— Let
\alpha\in\Phi_{n}
,

w_{\alpha}\in W
and
R_{n}(\varrho)
be as in Lemma
9.3
,
for
n
odd, or as in Lemma
9.7
, for
n
even. ​Then:

a). If
n=2k+1
, we have

R_{n}(\varrho)\overline{f_{w_{\alpha}}}(\underline{1},\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2};\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle-n/\penalty 2})=R_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\varrho)\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-k}}(\underline{1},\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2};\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle-n/\penalty 2})\cdot v_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}

where we recall that
R_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\varrho)=\left(\varrho;\varrho\right)_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle-11}
.

b). If
n=2k+2
, we have

R_{n}(\varrho)\overline{f_{w_{\alpha}}}(\underline{1},\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2};\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle-n/\penalty 2})=R_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\varrho)\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-k}}(\underline{1},\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2};\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle-n/\penalty 2})\cdot\overline{f_{\sigma_{\scalebox{.67}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}t}}(\underline{1},\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2};\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})

where we recall that

R_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\varrho)=\left(\varrho;\varrho\right)_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle-8}\left(\varrho;\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\right)_{\infty}^{\scriptscriptstyle-6}(1-1/\penalty\varrho)^{\scriptscriptstyle-7}
.
We also have

\overline{f_{\sigma_{\scalebox{.67}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}t}}(\underline{1},\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2};\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})=\,\!^{{}^{t}}\!\!\left(\tfrac{\varrho^{4}+7\varrho^{3}+13\varrho^{2}+7\varrho+1}{\varrho^{4}},\tfrac{\varrho^{3}+7\varrho^{2}+7\varrho+1}{\varrho^{7/\penalty 2}},\tfrac{3\varrho^{2}+7\varrho+3}{\varrho^{3}}\right)\!.

Proof.

The identities follow by expressing

\mathbf{C}_{\alpha,\,1}(\underline{x};u)

in two ways, using (
49
) and Lemma
9.13
. After
can-celling the singular parts, one can then evaluate at

\underline{x}=\underline{1}
by using Lemma
9.3

(resp. Lemma
9.7
) for
n
odd(resp. ​
n
even),
and Lemma
9.14
.
∎

We are now ready to show the following:

Proposition 9.16
.

— For
n\geqslant 1
, put

\varrho=q^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty$}n}<1
.
Then the leading coefficient of
Q_{n}(D,q)
is

(-1)^{\lfloor n/\penalty 2\rfloor}\varrho^{3\lfloor n/\penalty 2\rfloor\cdot\lfloor(n+1)/\penalty 2\rfloor}\cdot g_{\scriptscriptstyle n,D}(\sqrt{\varrho})

where
g_{\scriptscriptstyle n,D}\neq 0
is a power series with non-negative
coefficients depending only upon
\lfloor D/\penalty 2\rfloor

modulo
n
.

Proof.

​As before, put

k=\lfloor(n-1)/\penalty 2\rfloor
.
When
k=0
(i.e.,
n=1,2
), our assertion can be verified
directly fromProposition
9.12
and
Corollary
9.15
; the cocycle
\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-k}}
is the
identity matrix in this case. If
k>0
, we first observe that the
entries of the matrix

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-k}}(\underline{1},\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2};\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle-n/\penalty 2})

in Corollary
9.15
are of the form

(-1)^{k}\varrho^{3k(n-k)}

times non-zero power series in
\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2}

with non-negative coefficients. ​Indeed, by the cocycle relation,
we can write

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-k}}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};1/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle n})=\tilde{B}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};1/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle n})\tilde{B}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};1/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle n-2})\cdot\ldots\cdot\tilde{B}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};1/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle n-2k+2})

and note that
n-2k+2\geqslant 3
. Now, for
i\geqslant 3
, Lemma
A.1

gives the functional equation

\tilde{B}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};1/\penalty x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle i})=-\,x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 6(i-1)}\cdot\,\!^{t}\!\tilde{B}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle i-2}).

(50)

By Theorem
4.3
, the function

\tilde{B}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};u)
has poles only if

ux_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{j}=\pm 1
(
j=0,1,2
), and so both sides of
(
50
)are defined.
​Furthermore, by Proposition
A.2
, the entries of the matrix in the
right-hand side of (
50
) are non-zero power
series in
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}
with positive coefficients, and thus the
assertion about

\tilde{\Lambda}_{\tau^{-k}}(\underline{1},\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2};\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle-n/\penalty 2})

fol-lows at once by taking
x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}=\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2}
and
summing the exponents
of
\varrho
coming from (
50
).

The remaining factors in the right-hand sides of the formulas
in Corollary
9.15
are also power series with non-negative
coefficients, except for the factor

(1-1/\penalty\varrho)^{\scriptscriptstyle-7}=-\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\left(1+\varrho+\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}+\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 3}+\cdots\right)^{\scriptscriptstyle 7}

in the formula of
R_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\varrho)
. ​Thus, when
n
is even,
the product

-\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 7}\overline{f_{\sigma_{\scalebox{.67}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}t}}(\underline{1},\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2};\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle-1})

gives an additional factor of
(-\varrho)^{\scriptscriptstyle 3}
. Finally, to see
that the series obtained after applying Proposition
9.12

is not identically zero, onejust needs to notice that the
power series of
R_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(\varrho)
and
R_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(\varrho)
contain
powers
\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle j}
with non-zero coefficients for all residue
classes
j
modulo
n
(coming for example from the expansion
of
(1-\varrho)^{\scriptscriptstyle-1}
). This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 9.3
.

​The estimate of the power of
\varrho
dividing the leading
term in the previous proposition is essentially optimal.
To see this, let us assume that
D
is even and that

D/\penalty 2\equiv 3\lfloor n/\penalty 2\rfloor\cdot\lfloor(n+1)/\penalty 2\rfloor\pmod{n}
.
Then the leading coefficient of the polynomial
Q_{n}(D,q)
,
expanded as a power series in

\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 1/\penalty 2}=q^{\scriptscriptstyle-1/\penalty 2n}
,
is asymptotically

(-1)^{\lfloor n/\penalty 2\rfloor}\varrho^{3\lfloor n/\penalty 2\rfloor\cdot\lfloor(n+1)/\penalty 2\rfloor}(1+O(\varrho))\cdot\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}n^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 10$}}}\prod_{j=0}^{3}\frac{(2j)!}{(4+j)!}&\text{ if $n$ odd}\\
\frac{1}{7!n^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 7$}}}&\text{ if $n$ even}.\end{cases}

Indeed, by Lemma
5.3
, one has
\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};0)=\tilde{B}_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}
, and
the formula follows at once from the argument of the proof of the
previous proposition, combined with Proposition
9.12
and the
formulas at the beginning of this subsection.

Appendix A
Properties of the matrix
B(\mathbf{x};u)

We collect here two properties of the matrix
B(\mathbf{x};u)
in
Theorem
4.3
that were needed in the proofs
of Corollary
5.5
and Proposition
9.16
.

From the explicit formula of the matrix

B(\mathbf{x};u)=A^{-1}(\mathbf{x};u\mathbf{x}^{\delta})
,
with
A
given in
[
1
]
, one verifies the follow-ing
functional equation.

Lemma A.1
.

— The matrix

\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u)=B(\mathbf{x};u)/\penalty(1-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}x^{\delta})^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
satisfies

{}^{t}\!\tilde{B}\big(\mathbf{x};1/\penalty u\mathbf{x}^{\delta}\big)=-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 6}\mathbf{x}^{{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\delta}\tilde{B}(\mathbf{x};u).

The main result of this appendix is the following:

Proposition A.2
.

— Each entry of

\tilde{B}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};u)
is of the form

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a,b,c\geqslant 0\\
a+b+c=11\end{subarray}}\frac{p_{\scriptscriptstyle a,b,c}(x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5},u)}{\left(1-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}}\!\left(1-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle b$}}\!\left(1-u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\right)^{\scalebox{1.1}{$\scriptscriptstyle c$}}}\neq 0

where
p_{\scriptscriptstyle a,b,c}(x,y)
are polynomials with non-negative coefficients.

Let
\varrho=x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5}^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
,
q=u^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
, and set
r=\varrho q
.
We have:

\tilde{B}(\underline{1},x_{\scriptscriptstyle 5};u)=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{b_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 11$}}}{(q-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}(r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 11$}}(\varrho r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}&\frac{\sqrt{r}\,b_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 12$}}}{(q-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}(r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 11$}}}&\frac{\sqrt{q}\,b_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 13$}}}{(q-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}(r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 10$}}(\varrho r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}\\[8.0pt]
\frac{\sqrt{r}\,b_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 21$}}}{(\varrho r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}(r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 11$}}}&\frac{b_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 22$}}}{(r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 11$}}}&\frac{\sqrt{\varrho}\,b_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 23$}}}{(\varrho r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}(r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 10$}}}\\[8.0pt]
\frac{\sqrt{q}\,b_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 31$}}}{(q-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}(r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 10$}}(\varrho r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}&\frac{\sqrt{\varrho}\,b_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 32$}}}{(q-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}(r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 10$}}}&\frac{b_{\scalebox{.7}{$\scriptscriptstyle 33$}}}{(q-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}(r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 9$}}(\varrho r-1)^{\scalebox{.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle 4$}}}\end{pmatrix}

where the numerators
b_{\scriptscriptstyle ij}=b_{\scriptscriptstyle ij}(\varrho,q)
are explicit
non-zero polynomials, given in
[
1
]
. The proposition
fol-lows immediately from the following decompositions
of
b_{\scriptscriptstyle ij}
.

Lemma A.3
.

— We have

b_{\scriptscriptstyle 22}(\varrho,q)=p_{\scriptscriptstyle 22}(r)
,
where the polynomial
p_{\scriptscriptstyle 22}
has negative coefficients,
and the other numerators decompose as follows

b_{\scriptscriptstyle 11}=\vec{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle 8}\cdot\vec{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle 11},\quad b_{\scriptscriptstyle 12}=\vec{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\cdot\vec{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle 12},\quad b_{\scriptscriptstyle 21}=\vec{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\cdot\vec{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle 21},

b_{\scriptscriptstyle 13}=(q-1)\varrho\vec{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}\cdot\vec{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle 13}+(r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\vec{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\cdot\vec{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle 13},\quad b_{\scriptscriptstyle 31}=(\varrho r-1)\vec{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}\cdot\vec{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle 13}+(r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\vec{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle 4}\cdot\vec{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle 13},

b_{\scriptscriptstyle 23}=\vec{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\cdot\vec{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle 23},\quad b_{\scriptscriptstyle 32}=\vec{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}\cdot\vec{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle 32},\quad b_{\scriptscriptstyle 33}=\vec{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}\cdot\vec{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle 33}

in terms of the vectors

\vec{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2k}=\left[(r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle 2k-2i}(q-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle i}(\varrho r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle i}\right]_{i=0,\ldots,k},

\vec{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}=\left[(r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle k-i}(q-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle i}\right]_{i=0,\ldots,k},\quad\vec{f}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}=\left[(r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle k-i}(\varrho r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle i}\right]_{i=0,\ldots,k}

where
\vec{p}_{\scriptscriptstyle ij}
and
\vec{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle 13}
denote vectors
of non-zero polynomials with negative coefficients
of the same size as the first vector in the scalar products.

Proof.

The decompositions can be verified using the explicit
formulas of the vectors
\vec{p}_{ij}
and
\vec{q}_{\scriptscriptstyle 13}

in
[
1
]
. By Lemma
A.1
, the polynomials

b_{ji}(\varrho,q)
can be expressed in terms of

b_{ij}(\varrho,1/q\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 2})
, and so we only have to consider half
of the off-diagonal entries of
\tilde{B}
. We illustrate how one
arrives at these decompositions by considering an example.

For the entry
b_{\scriptscriptstyle 33}
, we notice that

b_{\scriptscriptstyle 33}(\varrho,1/\varrho^{n})=(\varrho-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle 6}g_{n}(\varrho)

for
n=0,1,2,\ldots
, where
g_{n}
are Laurent poly-nomials with negative coefficients. Therefore we look for a
decomposition involving the factors in the de-nominator of the
(3,3)
entry of the form:

b_{\scriptscriptstyle 33}(\varrho,q)=\sum_{i=0}^{3}(r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle 6-2i}(q-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle i}(\varrho r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle i}h_{i}(r)

with
h_{i}(r)
polynomials in
r=\varrho q
. (This assumption on

h_{i}
and the type of decomposition is suggested by the
symmetry of the diagonal entries under

q\mapsto 1/\penalty q\varrho^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}
.) Under this assumption,
the polynomials
h_{i}
can be determined recursively,
by letting
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(r)=b_{\scriptscriptstyle 33}(r,1)/\penalty(r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle 6}
,
and repeating the same procedure with
b_{\scriptscriptstyle 33}(\varrho,q)
replaced
by

\frac{b_{\scriptscriptstyle 33}(\varrho,q)-b_{\scriptscriptstyle 33}(r,1)}{(q-1)(\varrho r-1)}

etc. All the coefficients of the polynomials
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(r)

and
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 3}(r)
thus found are already negative, and only
twoof the coefficients of
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(r)
and
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(r)

are positive. ​To eliminate the latter, we replace
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(r)
and

h_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(r)
in the above decomposition by

h_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}^{\prime}(r)=h_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(r)-(r-1)^{\scriptscriptstyle 2}(r+r^{\scriptscriptstyle 7}),\quad h_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{\prime}(\varrho,q)=h_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}(r)+(r+r^{\scriptscriptstyle 7})(q-1)(\varrho r-1)

so that
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 2}^{\prime}(r)
has now all coefficients negative.
​In the same way, we replace
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{\prime}(\varrho,q)
and

h_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}(r)
by
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{\prime\prime}(\varrho,q)
and
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}^{\prime}(\varrho,q)
, respectively, so that
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 1}^{\prime\prime}(\varrho,q)
has only negative
coefficients, and find that
h_{\scriptscriptstyle 0}^{\prime}(\varrho,q)
has onlynegative coefficients as well.

The same method can be used to decompose the entries

b_{\scriptscriptstyle 11}
,
b_{\scriptscriptstyle 12}
and
b_{\scriptscriptstyle 32}
in terms of the
entries of the corresponding vector
\vec{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle 2k}
or

\vec{e}_{\scriptscriptstyle k}
, as in the statement of the lemma. For

b_{\scriptscriptstyle 13}
, we decompose both

(b_{\scriptscriptstyle 13}(\varrho,q)\pm b_{\scriptscriptstyle 31}(\varrho,q))/\penalty(\varrho\pm 1)

in terms of the entries of
\vec{a}_{\scriptscriptstyle 6}
, with coefficients
that are again polynomials of
r
alone. We recover

b_{\scriptscriptstyle 13}(\varrho,q)
as a linear combination of these decompositions.
∎
